A,

To the Memory Of My Mother

DEATH SANDS

CHAPTER I 

OLD merciful Sol, with all his majestic rays of splendor, had sunk below the western horizon of Old Morocco. This country, it seems, had for countless ages dealt with the strangeness and mysteriousness of life. Life wrapped up in silence,-life wrapped in such a manner, that it would seem nothing to a creature if he should become lost in the labyrinths of this deep and darkened country. In this world, there were many whose lives seemed as nothingshattered nerves, and wrecked spirits; yet, above this there were creatures whose personalities stood out in contrast to the rest.

It was the year 1919, just one year after the ending of the World War. France, the authoritative nation of 

North Africa, had seen it well worth while to govern her dependencies in the strictest manner. Among them was the country of Morocco. She had been having troubledire trouble, with the natives within her borders. Troops had been sent here, men whose lives were embedded into the rank and file of the Foreign Legion,-men whose souls beat and kept time with that memorable group.

The year 1919 had been eventful for France. Her soldiers had been shifted here and there, in order to keep the nation from falling to pieces. Such a group we find on their way, just marching,-marching. The sun, on this day, had given its last salute to old mother earth, and the fast approaching twilight covered the sands and people hereabouts with a blanket of darkness. Five companies of men were traveling with all their might to a fortress. 

Roughly estimating, these five companies numbered four hundred and fifteen,-four hundred of the worst wrecks of the civilized world gathered into one compact body. 17.

DEATH SANDS 

Yet, all had sworn allegiance to this nation and were willing to do her bidding. The officers were practically all bullies, for none other could govern such a group. On they marched! The rat-a-tat-tat and the booming of drums drowned each yelp and cry, for it was a Legion of the brave and only the strongest survived. 

As the drum beat a stroke, it hid some derelict's yelp.

Perhaps it was for the best since mutiny might result, and the officers couldn't stand for that-Where were they?

For all they knew they might be in hostile parts. They couldn't afford to use their trusty weapons on these men in order to quiet them. The steps of these foot-sore légionnaires lagged, their bodies barely caught the motion of the march, but their faces were stern under the severe ordeal. The steady beating of muffled drums seemed to create a pep within them,—a pep which personified some thing higher than what they were accustomed. The drums reminded them they were soldiers and not mere beasts of burden worming their way in and out among the sand dunes. Stubborn souls had trod these sands, but not the existing type which is rather so common among the barbaric tribes of North Africa; especially not so with the tribes around the vicinity of Camp Kirzah. Night was fast approaching, the desert varnish had begun to make its appearance upon the desert floor, and the little moisture within the sands seemed to ooze up to the surface and meet the cool atmosphere above. 

Again, off in the distance, the never ending beating of drums and the treading of men reëchoed. A well trained outfit, but appallingly shabby, though their shabby and bedraggled appearance was not unusual. In fact, the climate was such that it weathered and weakened clothes as it sapped and crippled the vitality of these men; so that life mattered little to them. Yet, it destroyed not that one thought in their minds,-the oath to France, and their desire to strengthen that alliance. 

Taldemalt, one of the awe-inspiring vales of this coun-

DEATH SANDS 

9 try, was unfolding before them,-still just a panorama of sand, but nevertheless interesting, a picturesque plateau surrounded on all sides by mountains of sand. Seemingly through the years this valley had not suffered the rampages of moving sand dunes. It remained always the same. 

A true desert, sparsely dotted with a few grasses and shrubs of this country. Like the French soldiers these little plants called out for a meager supply of water to keep their fast wilting lives awake. Their growth was dwarfed. In outward aspects, the soulless wretches of this Legion were lucky. They had powers of locomotion, could continue their struggle for existence, while the plants and shrubs of Taldemalt could hardly exist. Would these men survive in this clime? Would they come up to the nature of human beings after a while in the hot burning desert of Morocco? That remained. A strangeness indeed surrounded the whole affair, but this is Morocco-the great desert of the Northern Sahara.

Again the troops came nearer and nearer. Their lives were shrouded with an ominous silence. Tattered ranks, souls burned and tarnished while a little resentment on their part would make beasts of practically all. They had been trudging so far and yet not their destination reached. 

Tired aching feet plodded through sand driven paths, while a dark tropical night hovered over them like a warm, thick blanket. Bloodshot eyes were rested now. Thoughts of their former lives surged through their minds. These thoughts gripped each one in different ways. It is true melancholy moods seized practically all and reigned supreme. Many had been forced to give up family and friends, just to land in this God forsaken place,—just to continue this march of death to a destination, which to many seemed only a mystery enshrouded by a blanket of hate suspicious devils, these wrecks. They hated their fellow men, despised the lot which had thrown them here, wished and longed for a happiness that never could be. For freedom, they had given so much. They were willing to

DEATH SANDS 

segregate themselves from society to keep their meager bodies from rotting in some continental prison. Yes, life had dealt a queer hand of cards to these miserable pawns. 

Here they were, off in a desert clime, forced to endure the condition of such a country. Officers like men had received their experience in the school of life, and had their reasons for being here as well. Perhaps this fellow had committed some burglary on the continent. Maybe this one had killed a man-but why should that matter, questions are never asked in the Legion. Maybe this officer had won disfavor at home. Perhaps he was in some scandal over a fellow officer's wife, at least he's a handsome man that women would take a fancy to. Maybe a killing had fallen to his lot, too. Nevertheless, all had their reasons, and it was useless to try to analyze them no matter how hard one would try to contemplate. Odd, that such a group of men should congregate here. It was their lot and they must continue soldiering for five years-five long years in the dry heat ridden climate of Morocco. Yet, strange to say, it was this infernal heat escaping from the bowels of the earth which drove one to agonizing thoughts inhuman of people; but the life of this Legion lived on and on. A little marching accompanied with singing, singing of that immortal song of the Foreign Legion-a song written in such a way that it would drive men's souls to the very skies. 

It was a brave song which leapt with joy from their throats. It was a high-born song that kept France in supremacy of these desert wastes. Each scuffle of shoes along these desert sands reverberated with the spirit of this song. A march in unison, all as one machine, brought vigor to their steps as this ne'er-forgotten air was sung. 

Their voices melted into final strains. Again they cursed their own thoughts of being here; but why should they worry. It was to their benefit to be here. An oath; then a patriotic air to liven their spirits, and remind them of their pledge to France. It seemed to grip them and strengthen their steps. The retorts of the lower officers

DEATH SANDS 

11 frequently sallied over the deadened Moroccan night. A husky voice broke the silence of the night-a barking voice. "Hey ya bloomin' idiots, git a wiggle on yez. Do yez think we wants ta git caught here I say no. Faster I tell yez,-faster fools, move! Do ya think yer runnin' a race with snails ?"

The group set forth with a little more fervor. It was the harsh voice of their Top Sergeant and in unison they answered, "Ya, comin', Sarg.' 99 "Is that all yer sayin'," returned the brutal voice. "Look, ya fellers, see the moon over head? By the time it reaches mid-sky I want yez devils ta be two miles from here." "Impossible," came a protesting group of voices. "Listen, ye square-heads, no back talk. Hasn't the high mogul, Colonel La Tour, given orders? Bless his soul. 

Why yez brats ought ta be thankful yer livin'. Ya know orders are not slight affairs and if I hev ta enforce 'em I will. I stand fer discipline. In the rear there, what's ya sayin'? Not opposin' are ya? Speak up Corporal Tait, it's yer turn, and speak damn quick!” "I don't mean to spread trouble. Why man, you can well expect to make two miles on horseback but the devils on foot-do you know what that means? Double quick time and then some. 99 "So ya think we can't do it, Tait. Ha-ha-ha! Ye runt, ya stand there talkin' to me and makin' me an object of ridicule. Never, nothin's impossible with Top Sergeant O'Leary." "Yes?" "Step out a ranks! Come over here ta the horse." "Not that you have to listen, only reasonable. It will be in mid-sky in a jiffy and we can't make it." "Ya think yer smart, Tait. Let this soak thru ya. I think yer a sissy. Not worth a damn. How ya got them chev'erns is a mystery to me. "I earned them, sir." 99 "Shut yer damned mouth," shouted O'Leary, "I'm talkin' and you listen. Since leavin' the headquarters, I didn't like ya. Yer too big, ya gab ta much. Yer an ornery buzzard, and I always said the General Office gave me the hardest bunch of idiots ta drill. Why none of yez fools can march without stumblin' over yer feet. Up,-up I tell yez,-git goin'. Clever chap ya are Tait. Damned clever 

Yankee! Talkin' to me, why I'm an officer and Top Sergeant of this outfit.” "Yes, sir." "Ye better 'yes sir' me," barked O'Leary reeling forward on his horse. "Yes, sir." "See the fist? It's seen many a battle, Tait, many a battle and it's still good. Why it was given ta me ta discipline the blocks the General Office gives me ta drill. See, yer no man. It takes muscle ta make one of 'em and that's me. Lucky yer a kid; but that doesn't stop me from hittin' ya to give ye a little kindly discipline," spoke O'Leary leaning a little further over in the saddle. 

O'Leary quickly slid from his horse and faced Tait.

Glaring at his own fist, then at Tait's, he laughed. Certainly not the calloused fist of a légionnaire! Something about the fellow had made him laugh, but this soon gave way to a scowl as he shot his fist forward and caught Tait under the chin. Nothing queer about this. O'Leary was the disciplinarian of the companies and orders had been given, "no back talk to the officers." The blow dealt Tait had been severe, had knocked his feet from under him and sent him sprawling to the ground. The incident had furnished sheer delight to O'Leary.

DEATH SANDS 

13 "Thar,-thar ya fool, crawl,-crawl on yer belly like a snake. That's all any of ya are. Crawl, ya fool, crawl. 

See what happens when yez try to override me. That's what I do. O'Leary the pride of the legion. Ha-haha- "Stop!" came a challenging voice of hate. "Listen 'ere buddy, ya needn't treat the fella like that. 'E was lookin' after our welfare. Why-'e's only a kid and didn't mean wrong. Ya beat and kick us all around. Some day O'Leary even though 'ate 'ides yer soul, yer goin' ta like this chap. 

Ever since ya met up with 'im at Saida ya've made ’im a goat. 'E's jest as good an' probably better than any of us; there's no need for all of this. I'm in 'is patrol, and I like the fella, like 'is guts—at least 'e's a man and not yella. Cheerio, Sergeant, cheerio and that will be all from me. 99 

With a surprised look O'Leary, the self-styled pride of the Legion surveyed the foot-soldier from head to toes. 

Then opening his mouth in astonishment said, "Ya, my word, yer talkin' ta me," he sneered. "What a damn surprise, a bloomin' private, ha-ha-ha!" "Maybe a private, my deah Sergeant, but a man who 'as a soul. Some time if I 'ad the gloves hon, old deah, I'd show ya." "What?" “I—I say, ya cawn't scare me, O'Leary, cause I'm not the scarey kind." "An yer name," remarked O'Leary with great gusto. "Jest "Gentleman' John." “A name, and what a fine one fur a man in your fix. A gentleman in the Legion—a gentleman. Ha-ha-ha!" "Ya seem to class yerself in a 'igh plane, my 'onorable 

Top Sergeant," John snapped back sarcastically. "Pokin' fun at an officer?" yelped O'Leary. "No, jest talkin'," "Gentleman' John retorted. "Anyway, ya surely hev the earmarks of a gentleman.

Sounds like a title ya gave yerself. If ya've ever been a

DEATH SANDS 

fine feller; well I've been a king. Yet yer not afraid. Most men shrink with fear when seein' me." ""Owever, not me," John uttered with a nonchalant manner. "Ya don't say. We heven't time for trouble; so I'll be on my way." "Cheerio, old thing, 'ere ye've dropped yer 'ankie." "What was that?" O'Leary uttered half turning. "One a the men dropped 'is 'ankie."

Tait the grave and dauntless Corporal of this Legion lay on the ground. Just one blow had sent him to the realms of the unconscious. A dead silence took possession of the men. The soldiers now had fastened their eyes to the front. John, Tait's one friend, was engaged in the task of trying to bring him back to his senses. The Corporal's head lay on his knee, signs of consciousness began to creep over his face as John slapped and fanned it vigorously. "Up-up, Tait," came John's whispering voice.

A légionnaire next to John had heard the conversation, so he too showed his kindness. "Come on, buddy, rise-throw out the proud chestraise her up-I jest saw one of the officers lookin' back this-a-way and ya know what he'll do." "Oh-o-oh my feet, John, they're heavy and I can hardly lift them. Look at my jaw, is there a bump there-it feels queer." "Ya, the bully popped ya a good one" "An my head feels like it's paralyzed." "Never ya mind 'im, 'is time will come." "Anyway he swatted me like a prize fighter." "Ah, git up, Tait, I'm 'elpin' ya.' 99 "There, I guess it's all right. Probably will have to go around with that protruding bump for some days." "Yer feelin' fine, can ya walk straight-no fear a stumblin'?" "I can manage myself all right. Thanks for helping me,

DEATH SANDS 

15 it was awfully nice of you. You're one of my few friends here, and I'm glad to have your friendship." "Don't ya care, chappie. Never ya worry, we're not yer kind. Why yer better 'en any a us," John exclaimed in an appreciative manner as they resumed their marching. 

Eyes front, feet placed in a correct angle, and the grinding shoes of the men again forged through the sand. Here the need of happiness exerted itself by the pulsating throbs and the beating of weary feet over the coarse grained sands. 

A steady tatoo of feet continued over the driven path,—a steady, stern look greeted the surrounding country. A few vultures of the desert seemed to lag behind the companies and keep pace with the weary légionnaires. Just birds of prey, but maybe to them some wretch would drop; then they could start their death work. 

Officers were hardened, severe shouts leapt from their throats, yet they couldn't be blamed for maltreating these men. A climate such as this can try men's souls, make wretches of them, and cause them to lose all self-respect. 

Sand had crept in through their badly worn shoes and had shifted between the toes. It grated against the flesh and drove them to madness. They had marched so far on this day, their minds were weary and they couldn't keep track of time; so on-on they crept. The time was perhaps nine 

P. M. The moon had just drifted beyond mid-sky. Even so they must keep going, for there was no rest for the weary here. They were true soldiers,-true soldiers of 

France, and a few tears and hurts meant nothing. Only the oath taken on joining meant anything. "For the honor and glory of France, I give my life." A strange oath for such a group of men to accept, and they doubted whether they could live up to it. Regardless of this, they could,would live up to this hypnotic vow and stave off all weariness for the sake of France. France the supreme,-France the soul of authoritative nations of North Africa. Another command broke the stillness of this eve,-another command which meant only words to most of them. They were ex-

DEATH SANDS 

hausted, half crazed and couldn't quite understand the order of things. "Attèncion," came the soft, clear voice of one of the upper officers.

Still a silence gripped the men, their mouths flew open in astonishment. They couldn't quite comprehend; or even listen to these commands. A dazed feeling had struck them. 

Again a voice rang through the air. The harsh and brusque voice of Top Sergeant O'Leary. "Hey brats," rang out O'Leary, "brace up. What's the matter with yez? Another command and yez stand thar jest like dumb asses." "But-but-" protested the voices in unison, "we're tired." "Tired eh-yer tired. What's this, a group of sissy gals on a hike? Listen, now listen ya buzzards; no back talk. 

Oive seen miny a company of min but none like yez devils. 

Why a more cut-throat gang I niver did see. What yez sayin'?-What yez sayin'? Nothin'? Then damn it, snap to attention when an officer speaks. He's restin' yez bloakes when he gives the command attention." 

Words trickled through some tired brains. To others they meant nothing. Some had grown stark mad. A mad fool, one fellow in particular, his feet seemed to drift, his eyes were fixed in a staring glare, and his teeth just seemed to grind on-and on. The poor fellow was sick, but this was not the home of sick men-it was the place of the strong. A poor chap just mad, nothing less,-nothing more. Commands meant nothing to him, and his voice reached maniacal proportions as he shrieked. "Ya, it-it's attention, we're havin' now, it's attention! 

Yes-ya-yes it's attention! Why we're restin'! Are we restin'? Ya we're restin'. We're not goin' ta die, are we? 

No, we're not goin' ta die-ha-ha, huh-huh, ha-ha-ha. I— 

I-I told ya, ya old buzzard we wouldn't die." "Hey, yez," broke out O'Leary in a tone of anger. "Lead

DEATH SANDS 

17 the loon away. He's crazy and I don't care to hev any of the rest git like him." "Yes, sir," returned a légionnaire with a quick salute. "Attention the rest of yez. What's wrong, don't ya know commands. Stop the laggin' or maybe I'll come through the ranks and paste every one of yez. Yez won't like that."

Attention, the snappy command rang out. This order meant much. A quickened jerk, and the men took on a stiffened appearance. This time the officer in charge of the five companies, the new commandant, had given an order. 

Colonel La Tour, a man who stood for honor, integrity and everything dear to the nation he served. A strange personality this Colonel. Rumors had it, that he had come to this desert in his early days, had worked himself up from a private to the high office he now held. Other rumors were that he wanted adventure; so when a young fellow he went in quest of it. The Legion, however, claimed him. He had lived the life of these men, had grown up with the force and had given his best years to its service. On his sensitive 

Arabian charger he looked every inch the officer he was. 

Yet, there was something about the man which seemed to make one observe him. For a man of his years he was well preserved. His sharp, piercing eyes seemed to grip everything within their reach. La Tour had remained always the same. Always the calm man who settled difficulties among his fellow légionnaires. Yes, he was a typical old war horse, who considered loyalty to France more than anything else. In discipline he was severe. He believed, orders should be taken. This then was the man talking. 

The beloved Colonel La Tour of the Foreign Legion. It was a short brief order he gave, but it meant so much to these soul weary fellows. "Tonight, men," spoke La Tour in a calm steady voice, "you rest. We've marched you such a great distance, and it is for one reason. We wish to make the Post as quickly

DEATH SANDS 

as possible. Perhaps it may be tomorrow. So, Messieurs, this is final. I ask you to break ranks."

With this order La Tour bowed, turned his horse and started away. To the men, La Tour was a kind commander. It was their Colonel who had spoken-one of them. So with shouts and hurrahs the men walked to their various duties. Some engaged in taking out supplies, others pitching pup-tents. "Hey, yez rats, come over here. Help us with this here load," barked the gruff voice of Sergeant O'Leary. "It's us, yer wantin', Sarg?" "Suppose I was pointin' and yellin' at Pole-cats?" "Oh no-o-o! You never yell, unless there's some one to yell at." "The Sergeant yelling at some poor fellow, Buddy," remarked Tait. "Yes," returned 'Gentleman' John as he finished beating the dust off his clothes. "I understand ye-yer just come over from the States ?" "Yes, that shouldn't seem queer. Why you look at me as though a deep crime has brought me here. It isn't strange. I'm even beginning to think I'll like it, even though the Sergeant continues yelling." "Yes, ya'll like it. Yer first trip into the desert, Tait?" "Why yes, John, I've been around the headquarters mostly." ""Eadquarters company, until ya were transferred to this 'ell 'ole?” "But I'm not kicking the bars here, why just a Corporal, you see I don't even know the reason I received them." "Tell me about it?" "Sorry I can't. Do you really think I'll like this place?

I-I hope so. Why I left-left, well that's nothing to moon over." 99 "There ya go again.

Brace up kid, ya remember my

Am I not a lad of twenty-five summers and the ladies used to tell me I was not hard to look upon." "A perfect Adonis, but ya've a lot to learn. My 'eavens, boy, the way ya talk I bet yer were a devil among the ladies." “I—I—oh, please please don't mention women to me; you know they're terribly boresome." "So yer bashful, jest like to make men think yer a sheik among 'em. I remember the last time in London; a lady-well was almost the cause of my downfall." "Then-then you're from London ?" Tait uttered in astonishment. "Ya, buddy, it's London Town from where I 'ail." "A truly wonderful city-very metropolitan and an oddity about the whole affair is that you Englishmen are so reserved and take life easy." "No, life is sort a slow on the Island, but then there are excitin' times. Where ya from, chappie?" "The middle portion of the U. S.” "A westerner-the part of ranches and Hindians ?" "No-no ranches from where I come; a lot of farmers in the place too, but the city isn't as metropolitan as your

Council Bluffs. I know you haven't heard of the other place, so let's drop the matter," Tait remarked in a saddened tone. "Oh, come on, let's git ta work or the officers will be comin' an beatin' us."

Rest, the much needed stimulant for all was upon them. 

Flickering campfires served to break the chill of the evening, as the men settled down to a slumber which meant rest for their aching limbs. Hurried steps of sentries, intermingled with the snoring of men seemed the only commotion in these parts. Near the fire, a sticky like heat prevailed. Moths and other desert insects were playing a game of pomp-pomp-pull-a-way with these men nearest the fire. Desert rats ran over them when flies subsisted their skirmishes. The gnawing of these rodents would keep an average person awake, but these men had grown accustomed to it.

"You see the nights are ordinarily cooler than this night." "Then it's unusual?” "Ya." "Hey, ya rats, over thar. Keep still. Camp fires out.

Dim the fires, I say. Orders are we're in dangerous territory," barked O'Leary. "The Sergeant." "Yeh, ya kin never mistake 'is voice." 

The coarse yelling of O'Leary had awakened all. Officers and men alike were angry but he was the disciplinarian; so what could they say. O'Leary had moved from the outer circle of the men, over towards the camp fire. "Do you mean we're liable to be attacked, Sergeant," spoke Tait in a highly excitable voice. "Why-why the baby lamb is probably anxious to make his git-a-way but not here." "Not that, I-I'm anxious for a fight," "Ya dirty dog, O'Leary, why the kid's brave, 'e's anxious to fight," spoke up John defensively.

DEATH SANDS 

"Again you speak, ya lousy private. Listen, oive yer words in mind. Don't furgit that." "Oh ya?" "Damn, the kid must hev nerve though." 66 "'E 'as a lot, O'Leary, more 'en me." "Keep quiet ya idiots, yer goin' ta sleep. We can't even sound the dern bugle for fear of rousin 'those damned 

Berbers curiosity. Lucky they don't know a relief is comin' to the fortress." "Good night, then, Sergeant," snapped John as O'Leary stamped away.

The solitude of the desert was a haven of rest to these men, but not for long because they must be up by five 

A. M. and continue marching.

Morning came all too suddenly. The sun had bathed his face and was waking up brighter than ever. His countenance fairly shone with radiance as he peeped over the eastern horizon. A strong cool wind was briskly blowing across the Taldemalt Plateau, a wind which made them shiver and shift positions; so that they might catch a few degrees of warmth. A few last dozes of sleep; then the ne'er-forgotten clarion blow of the bugle greeted their ears. Brutes must move; so the tired wretches stirred, yawned and awakened. A low call had sounded from the bugle clear, fresh notes, as though it too had been in slumber and had awakened to announce the coming dawn.

Sergeant O'Leary, the man who had rested the least of any, seemed always awake. On this morning he was at his usual task. Perhaps he suffered from insomnia and couldn't tolerate sleep in anyone else. To be a soldier one must be alert, ready to perform duties and obey orders at all times. O'Leary jumped to his feet and ran in and out amongst the men, kicking and punching them as he continued. "Hey! ya brats up-git goin'. What do ya suppose we're doin' all this for? This isn't the place for rest. Yer not society buds, remember that."

DEATH SANDS 

23 "We-we're comin', Sergeant."

Breakfast, the welcome meal of the army, consisted of only meager supplies. Coffee, hard tack, and a little swallow of sour wine. Practically nothing more than prison fare, but these men made the best of things. After mess, they were ready to form ranks. "Dern ya-ya can't even talk, kin ya? Why yer, yer a fine trooper, a fine légionnaire. See this, how me hands are clasped around one another? Put yer bloomin' head in there. Hurry up, dern ye." "Yes-yes, sir." "The Sergeant again, John." "Ya, buddy, one never mistakes 'is yelp." "Dang yez, I—I'll show yez-I'll show yez, we'll hev discipline ya buzzards.” "Please, please don't, Sergeant, I—I'm sick,” came the words of a lowly private in a begging voice. "Something you don't understand, buddy, it's different in my case."

The marching had started long ago. These men were hardly refreshed after the night's rest. Hope wavered in their eyes; feet struggled to march. Here they were marching on and on without fresh water in their canteens. What

DEATH SANDS 

25 would the effect be as this stale, stagnant water touched their parched lips? "Hey, ya fellas. Double time march," came the husky voice of the Top Sergeant.

O'Leary knew that these men were weakened through so much marching. He couldn't lose his brusque nature, for it was a part of him. The commissioned officers, too, knew that these men were sick. To La Tour, however, they seemed hardened. He had been thinking, had turned his head to one side, as he shifted his position in the saddle. Just recently he had been assigned to this new post. 

Here he was to relieve a Major Le Brande, who soon would finish his term of service away from civilization. Was La 

Tour to stay here five years? This thought crept into his mind, made him wonder, but time would tell. He had come to Morocco in his early years, had married; but where his wife and child were, he knew not. An unending search had been carried on by him, but with no result. As he sat meditating an under-officer approached him. 

Saluting and speaking in excited tones he said, "La 

Tour! La Tour!" "Well, what is it this time, my good man?" La Tour called out with a firm voice. "I-I why, Colonel! It's-it's ze men! Why they're loitering, angry. Dang it, it looks like beeg mutiny!" "No?" "And they're gaspin' for water an are sullen." "You say, they need water?" "Yes, look around, Colonel. Why, the recruits are stumblin'. They're gettin' ta hevey for their feet." "What about it?" "Look, that one back there he's, why the man, his eyes, zey are poppin' almost out-a his head. His tongue's hangin' out. Mon Dieu, Colonel! Look! He's droppin' on his knees an' draggin' his tongue over the sands as if he could find water. Please, let's rest!"

SANDS DEATH 

"Rest? then mutiny sure. It gives them time to think, besides we must make the place." "Where do we go?" "To the outpost Krim." "Krim ?" "Yes." "That, that hell-hole, why, ze-ze fortress, it has changed hands many a time.” "I know, but it won't when I'm there." "Help my com-panie, Colonel.” "Jacques-Jacques," Colonel La Tour shouted. He was calling his orderly. "Where is that fool anyway?" "Here, my Colonel." "Where have you been?" "At your side." "Ride back to Lieutenant Bridwell. Have his men taken to the end of the ranks, and the men at the rear placed near the middle." "Yes, Colonel, maybe we not be at Krim for long. Are you sure nothin' might happen. Ya know, these sands, they carry a queer death." "Go, you insolent fool!" "Yes, Colonel."

Lucky it was for La Tour and his men that Krim was near. Possibly, many more miles of marching would have brought mutiny. Off in the distance a murky shadow silhouetted against a clear sky revealed a fortress, a fortress of excitement and enchantment!

CHAPTER II 

FORT KRIM, that last desert stronghold of the French, stood in the desert like a wondrous jewel of enchantment. Enticement was to be the lot of these légionnaires for five long years. A spell had been cast about the fortress, some say, ever since its birth. The fort had fallen into various hands. First the Arabs held it; then the French. To the onlookers, the outpost was enfolded by bleak sombre looking walls. Sadness itself seemed manifest in those high, dry and sunbaked balustrades. A regular desert fortress; manned by martyrs who had not seen civilization for many years.

Marching on-on to a place just visible, was a welcome sight for the sore eyes of these men. Vultures played over head, flying and skirting the weary columns of soldiers. 

A warning of death! But were these birds here as forerunners of trouble and evil omens?

Sand was blowing slightly at first, but it grew stronger and stronger until a sandstorm, the most feared thing of the desert, was in progress. Still the men continued, a little sandstorm meant nothing to them. Clouds of sand swayed, -sprayed over the moving column of men and obscured their vision. A tremendous storm, sand-sand blowing in every direction! The fortress was now obscured. The excited voice of the Top Sergeant gave a harsh command. "Hey! ya fellas," barked O'Leary's voice. "If ya want to git inside the fort fore long git a wiggle on yez." "The fortress!" "Ya-." "An it's name, Sarg?" "Krim, it's Krim yer headin' for. The object in the distance is the place. Do yez see?" 27

"No, with this sand in our eyes." "Ha, yer a bunch of loons."

A tattered soldier spoke. Dreams had come to him,dreams which took on fantastic shapes. All through the day he had seen objects which would come and go,-like a picture that flits through a painter's mind. To this man, a real fortress was unbelievable. He couldn't understand; as he spoke in a quick broken voice. "Please, Sarg! I-I've been seein' objects all mornin'!

This-the-the-this tell me, it's Krim, isn't it? Don't tell me I'm loco. No-no-no, of course not, I'm not crazy.

Nothin' unusual, soldier, ya'll be doin' worse than that after yez been here a year or so.

-an ya woke me. Sort-a-wish I had, though, an yer tryin' hard to keep dry eyes.' "Yes." 99 "Sobbin' over me, too; well, I nearly passed away, but not quite. What was I doin' then ?" "Oh, nothin', brace up. See the fortress. We're just about ta Krim." "It-it'll be a fine place." "Hey, ya buzzards," called O'Leary, "double quick time, march! Don't crawl like snails."

Nearer and nearer came these exhausted ranks of men. 

Krim, their haven for the next five years, lay a few leagues further away. Joy crept over them, their legs moved just a little faster, their spirits were raised to higher levels. 

Now everything seemed better. Life took on a rosy hue. 

Views of the fortress quickened their steps. They wouldn't tire now. These men had been marching against time! it was to the benefit of the French government to get them here as quickly as possible. On -on they trudged, a walk which took on the aspects of a run. It was hard to keep them in ranks though they kept as orderly as possible. 

Officers alone knew the danger if some half-crazed creature should take a fancy to become leader of these men. Possibly annihilation of the whole group. Already they had been two weeks on the road, and the sudden joy of seeing this fort had driven most to the condition of imbeciles. 

They had run out of provisions, while their tempers had reached the limits of human endurance. Colonel La Tour, the commandant,-Sergeant O'Leary, and some of these other officers, who had been in the Legion many long years, knew these men. Had known the conditions of other men in a desert climate. As yet the valor of these soldiers hadn't been proven, but there was no doubt but what they'd be as brave as former légionnaires and would keep France supreme in the province of North Africa.

Sand driven by some power greater than life itself was blowing here and there. Sand and alkali crept into the

DEATH SANDS 

31 throats of these men, caused them to tear at their collars and call for water. Just a drink to quench their parched lips; just a drink to cause them to curse harder and damn the condition which brought them here. If hostile Berbers should attack them now, in their weakened condition, they couldn't fight.

Of this group, one seemed more accustomed to this hellhole than others. He seemed a part of this country. He seemed to enjoy it all. This fellow was La Tour's orderly. 

Odd, isn't it? Yes, his orderly was a Frenchman with popeyes which slanted like an oriental, and a black, silky moustache. His make-up blended with this place. Seemed more like a Moroccan than a white man. His existence caused a mysterious curiosity among the men. To La 

Tour he was known as Jacques. Jacques, the orderly; 

Jacques, the white man whose skin was yellow like an oriental poppy. It seemed queer that he accustomed himself so easily to this life. When asked questions concerning his life, he answered that he was born in Southern France, his younger years were spent in France. He was disliked and distrusted by officers and men alike, but that mattered little to Jacques. In fact, he enjoyed their hatred. "Hey, yez blubbers," called O'Leary. "Git a wiggle on yez, we've bin marchin' for hours an we're only a few rods from Krim. Can't yez step up a little?”

Fort Krim stood alone in that vast desert stillness. It had seen men come, struggle for existence and pass on to the valley of death. It had seen the desert chieftains, too, with their mighty hordes, how renegade tribes had journeyed over its enclosure; how chieftains had held possession of the place as their own. Krim alone knew the effect of the climate on these men; if it could only speak; ah, what tales it might tell!

Within the fortress things had taken on a decidedly different aspect. New life seemed to crop out here and there. Even the lone sentry pacing the southern balustrade stopped short in his path. Something was happen-

DEATH SANDS 

ing outside. His steps now quickened. He cupped his hand over his eyes thinking he had seen objects moving in the distance, but was not quite sure. With nervous zeal his hands found their way to his side. Picking up his field glass, he scanned the desert. The sandstorm had cleared away a little. No, it was not a mirage, whole companies of men were approaching. The relief forces of Krim! For weeks the légionnaires within Krim had expected relief. 

Now it was a reality, and the sentry stood and looked, limp with excitement. 

The Krim officers had thought these new relief forces lost; but yet, that couldn't be. Sergeant O'Leary, the man leading these new légionnaires, had been in the desert for years. In spite of his boasting, they knew his worth, knew he would come through. Again Sentry Tom, as he was known among his fellow-men, peered off into this murky haze. He couldn't comprehend. The French formerly had only one company of men here. Now they were sending more. Why were so many coming? Suddenly his eyes were fixed. The facial muscles were contorted. His mouth flew open and he gave way to a maniacal round of laughter. With insane gestures, he raised his gun and fired. "Hey-I-I hey! I s-s-say they're comin'-they're comin'-here-h-here already. Yes, we won't die-of course not-Ha-ha-ha-ha-huh-ha-huh!" "Who-who, Tom?"

Ha-ha-ha- "The-the-the Berbers. I-I knew it. huh-huh! They're comin' to kill us. Ya bet. They're comin' in waves, but they won't hurt us. No-no, I—I'll fight.

Ha-ha-ha-huh-huh-huh!” "Ah, yer mad, man. Stay on the parapet," came the voice of one of the lower officers. “I—I won't, I tell ya, I—I'm leavin'. Let me go. I- 

I'm goin' ta save myself. Ya fellas kin fight.”

In a frenzied shout, he dropped from the edge of the wall into the enclosure. Fired off into space, warning

DEATH SANDS 

33 the soldiers not to come near him. To him they seemed the Berber hordes. They hardly gathered his meaning, because through lack of comradeship their minds had been reduced to a child's understanding. They couldn't understand why new men were coming, couldn't believe that after so long a time they were to be given a chance for civilization-a chance to return, recuperate and strengthen their minds. To some, this meant freedom, a chance to leave the Legion with honor-a chance to quit French Morocco once and for all.

Officers were arriving to settle this commotion. Rapidly walking toward the group they were confronted by Old Tom, his mouth frothing, his face drawn, and talking in tones of excitement mingled with laughter, he shrieked "Ha-ha-ha-huh! Now he was gone-gone from the monotony of this life. Yes, it was monotonous to these men. A continuous

transfer from one post to another. Sentry Tom had made his last transfer. "Hey, the relief. They're comin'!" "Go on over and see what ze man, he wants!" "The relief army." "Open ze passageway, and ya wimmin, go over there!

The relief companies were here! At last they had reached their destination, the worst hell-hole in Northern Morocco! In through the barrier gate they poured, striding as giants!

Women hung on to the gates,-seemed fastened to the walls like climbing roses. Here were new men fresh from civilization to satisfy the whims of these painted dolls. 

Their white teeth gleamed as smiles radiated their faces, and their eyes sparkled and danced. These new légionnaires satisfied their fancy and with shouts of joy, they danced and drew their flowing garments towards their knees. The emotions of these men were stirred as they watched these Berber women execute the snake-like motions of the East. As a child throws aside a toy, so had these Berber women cast aside the old soldiers of Krim, and were exerting themselves to please these new and younger recruits. The soldiers were intrigued, women hadn't been on their menu for a month or more; and these 

Sahara roses weren't bad to look at.

The old soldiers within the fortress smiled as they watched the new men advance toward the center of the parade ground with the desert women following. They knew these women were seldom serious over sweethearts found outside Berber camps, and knew of their fickle

DEATH SANDS 

35 fancies. It was a relief to be released from such entanglements.

41 frivol away too much time with native women. It would be inhuman to deny them some time with these women of the desert; so they decided to allow them two hours a day. 

Time enough to satiate their desire for kisses.

La Tour and Le Brande were in the officers' quarters visiting. The ruse in getting rid of those women had worked and now the men went about their tasks. The scene now shifted to La Tour's quarters. "I say, Colonel La Tour, yer-yer, Ha-ha-ha-a 'markable soldier. I-I have a great hon-honor for thees man,' came Le Brande's half drunk voice. "Thank you, Major." "But come-come, jesh-jesh anosher drink-here gulp this down." "No, thanks, Major, it befuddles my brain. I advise any good soldier to leave it alone." "Damn it though, Colonel, ya jsh-jesh half-hev to drink here. I didn't like it eashur when I came here. The heat gives you a craze for drink. Here, drink-drink and be merry, forget your sorrow. Ya will fall sooner or later, jesh like me, Ha-ha-huh-huh-huh!" "My good man, you can never be a good judge of conditions when your brain is befogged with liquor.” "Brave talk, Colonel La Tour, but it takes a better man than I am." "Snap out of it, man-snap out of it! Why, a man of your calibre drinking, a major! It's a wonder hostile tribes didn't capture this fortress. Then it would be two losses, both yours and France's." "Jesh probably why they sent you here. Jesh 'curred to me, my drinking!" "Yes, they gave me no reason." "Whash that?" raising his hand. "I wouldn't do that, Major, I wouldn't like to arrest you." "Pardon, Colonel, I forgot myself. Have anosher drink. 

Then you go to your quarters and rest. You're drunk,

DEATH SANDS 

tomorrow you hash to relieve me. 

So long, I'm tired." "Goodbye, Major, I hope you recover with sleep." "Yesh, same to you." 99 The only evidence of life in Fort Krim was the sentries pacing the balustrades. Officers and men alike were taking a few hours' rest, while a hot tropical sun beat down on them, bringing a temperature of one hundred and ten.

CHAPTER III 

A нот tropical day had drifted down on the wretches of Fort Krim. The Berber women had been sent away. Possibly better for all concerned, if these women had never seen the légionnaires within the fort. Women were sometimes the causes offered for their being here; in other words, sometimes women are the existing causes for trouble. Maybe they would be here.

Women meant little to them now, since their one desire was sleep. Companies had been dismissed. Each poor devil doggedly wended his way to the different company barracks. It seemed as though some tremendous weight had suddenly been dropped on their shoulders, but on they trudged fairly dragging their guns. Low adobe like buildings confronted their eyes. These were to be their quarters for five long years. Certainly, there was nothing the least picturesque about this place; no sign of color. 

Just a dirty group of grey buildings and walls, the monotony of which almost drove one's mind to distraction. 

Darkness, broken only by a small penetrating ray of light which crept in through the doorway, met their eyes. 

This was barrack "A," the company and quarter of the famous Corporal Tait. A heavy, hinged door, which grated like the swinging of prison gates, separated the men from the outside. Off in the far corner of the room was an old wooden table with a small candle upon it, and scattered about were the uncomfortable beds. Moth-eaten comforters served as covers, no fine linens here! The men of company A were in the room and ready for sleep. Tait and John had chosen their bunks and were talking. "Our bed's over there, John," uttered Tait. 43

DEATH SANDS 

"Some boudoir, buddy, for five long years we'll be sleepin' 'ere. Between the beds and drill our backs will be broke." "Sh! The Sergeant." "Ya, probably makin' the rounds to see 'ow we like our new quarters." 

Sergeant O'Leary entered barrack "A" and gruffly called, "Hey, yez swine. Yer likin' these quarters, ain't yez? Tomorrow yer drillin' real hard, so take yer sleep." "These benches ain't any too soft, Sarg." "Ya ain't wantin' feather beds, are yez. The beds will feel good ta ya when yer bones are almost broke from drill." "Ya-a-a?" "What are ya lookin' at, John ?" "Nothin', can't outstare me, Sarg. I'm not afraid of ya cause yer only a Sergeant." "Maybe some day I'll be puttin' the gloves on with yez, then we'll see who's afraid." "Any time suits me, my deah Sergeant O'Leary." "God loves the Irish. He'll be on my side, be gorry!" "God loves the English, too!" "Ah, shut up, ya talk ta much. Go ta sleep, all of yez fools! Two hours is all ya git." 

Company "A" ceased talking; soon they ceased thinking, and the barrack was silenced by sleep. Sergeant 

O'Leary too had sought relief in sleep, but now the bugle summoned them to drill. "Hey, yez round-heads, up!" snapped O'Leary, entering the room. "Don't yez know it's drill time?" "So soon, Sergeant!" came a chorus of voices. "Ya, yez fools; we hev orders ta drill yez saps, taday and oi mean to do it. Hey ya over there, where's this 

Corporal Tait?” "Asn't awakened yet." "Git him up, fool, we don't show favors here." "Wake up, Tait," whispered John as he shook him gently.

45 "The damn sissy Corporal! Niver did like him. He'll larn what Legion life is—he'll larn!” "Yer jealous of 'im, Sergeant. 'E's better than ya are." "Better 'en me. Niver-a good man I was in me day, an a fair fighter." "Tait! Tait!” “Oh-oh-hum, what-what's that," spoke a sleepy voice. "I-I was dreaming." "Time for drill, buddy." "Yes, I was dreaming, John, dreaming of rest; actually of a nice comfortable bed! Funny for fellows like us to be dreaming of those things, when we're caught here like rats in a trap and are forced to make the best of it." "Hey ya two, quit chinnin'! We all dream. Come on ta drill. We'll all be heroes, Ha-ha-ha-ha-huh, heroes! 

I'll show ya how ta take the kinks out a yer back."

A swift, short blast of a bugle and sleep was supplanted by drill-monotonous, tiresome drill,-drill which would foul their bodies with perspiration. General orders were that they drill with full equipment. One by one they strapped on knapsacks and blankets; one by one they filed out of the barrack doors. Perhaps they were to add a twenty or twenty-five mile hike to their several hours of drill, but these outcasts dared not complain, they must feel grateful for their half-penny a day and their chance to be free from prison. Sergeant O'Leary, the fort disciplinarian, was well towards the front of these men. Lieutenant Bridwell, the French officer, uttered a command. "Attèncion." "Hey, yez lunkheads, move up inta line! Snap inta it! 

Don't yez hear the Lieutenant's voice! Got lead in yer shoes? Right dress; attention." "Sergeant O'Leary, the men are delivered to you. Take the fools an drill 'em. Start 'em out on somethin' light." "Ya, Lieutenant, a few steps around the parade ground, and" stepping up to Bridwell he whispered something.

Speak up, I'm waitin' fer a reply." "Just the gun, Sarg, it sorta bothered me like, andand 'sides, I'm tired." "Ya want ta rest?" "If ya please, Sergeant." "So yer the man who wants sleep and rest. Well, an

O'Leary moved toward the man. With a grinning leer, he struck him fair. O'Leary had surely given sleep to this surprised légionnaire, for now he was lying on the ground. No one moved or spoke in ranks for O'Leary was a strict disciplinarian. He was waiting for a reason to slap another man. "Squads right! March! One-two-one-two," rang the voice of O'Leary as he marched the men around the enclosure. "Halt! Ya remember the officer deliverin' yez min ta me. He told me ta drill yez. So I'm jest takin' yez fools on a twenty mile hike. Remember, the first 'un ta break down under this is goin' ta git somethin' he won't be lookin' for, come on-March!" "'E'll kill us, Tait," spoke John. "No, I don't believe so. Come, let's show him our speed." "Even marchin' us without our canteens, Tait. Some of the fellas won't pull through this." "Sh-sh, quiet. He's looking back this way. He's just testing our endurance. We'll surprise him and come through O. K." "One-two-one-two, step inta it min. Yer marchin', now, not standin' still. I'll march yez idiots. Ya fools will like drill after awhile. Ha-ha-ha-huh-huh!"

DEATH SANDS 

47 On-on, these plucky mortals of Krim marched. The sun was dipping in the western sky. The heat was unbearable, the hot winds seared the skin, particles of alkali penetrated noses and throats; together with all this, was the eternal thirst. O'Leary cared little that his men were stumbling, tearing at their throats and cursing the fate which had sent them here. He cared only that he might distinguish himself before the higher officers. One poor devil had fallen on his knees. "On yer feet, there, on yer feet! Yer not prayin'!" yelped O'Leary. "Curses, John, a man from my patrol! Wait, I'm stopping to help him." "It'll go mighty 'ard with ya, if the Sergeant sees ya. 'E'll about kill ya, but yer a bear for trouble." "Killing or no killing, I'm stopping." "Hey, Tait, what yez stoppin' for? Git back inta ranks." "I'm helping my buddy. He's crazed with thirst. He's lapping the sand!" "He won't find any water that way. Out of the way, Corporal, git back ta yer patrol. I'll treat this wreck. 

Shootin' is what he needs. It'll wake him up if I hit him in the leg. Any one else?" shouted O'Leary as he replaced his automatic in its holster and brushed his clothes. "Any one else who wants relief?"

A dead silence gripped the men. They were afraid of this creature and weren't going to take any chances with him. "Here, Tait, ya kin drag this man back ta camp.' "Yes, sir, Sergeant." "Back ta ranks ya bucks. March!" 99 A steady column of men was weaving in and out among the sand dunes of Taldemalt. with a stupefied stare; mouths gapped even further and called for water. The sun was finishing his work; but these wretches trudged on,-first stumbling, then rallying to the Foreign Legion song.

They marched and sang until now the gates of Krim were opened, and in marched the troops of légionnaires while the clarion blast of the trumpets announced their return.

CHAPTER IV 

THE afternoon march was over. The men had returned from the last tiresome hike of the day. Low in the flaming sky hung the mighty sun. Twilight was fast approaching.

Old Sol's face burned with a reddish glow, as though it pleased him to take leave until the morrow.

Indeed, it must be a relief from such a desolate place as Krim. These indwellers were spiritless tonight. The sensuous dances of the Berber women, with their passionate smiles, had charmed them for awhile. But now, a bitterness had taken possession of these souls. They were sad with gloom and misery as they huddled together, deprived of the simple pleasures of civilization. Here in barrack "A" was a representative group. Some had flung themselves across the beds; others sat upon the floor. They were listening to the plaintive tones of an old banjo. "Hey, , ya buddies, come, let's play, sing. Let's kill the monotony of this clime. Why, we'll be here for some time, an I tell ya it's gittin on my nerves." "What shall we play?" "For me," cried John, "my mind's weary, please strike up 'Danny Boy"." "Then 'Danny Boy' it'll be." "We fellas will all sing." "Sure an I'll 'elp ya fellas out. It might remind me a 'ome." "Fine, then, John. Join in." 

The song, "Danny Boy," took on form. A slow, sad rhythm sung by légionnaires who were full of heartaches, men whose eyes glistened with tears, men whose bodies shook with emotion. As the first few bars were sung, a 50

51

body lurched forward and yelled, "Stop-stop, G-d, yer killin' me." "And why?" uttered the banjo player. "I can't stand it but I must. Sing on, old things." 

Again the singing started and John rushed back to throw himself on his bed. He was possibly thinking of the times spent in Old London. How he used to sit by his mother's side and hear her sing this old song. The words had choked him with grief, and filled his heart with gloom. "John, buck up." "Tait! Tait!" "You're crying." "I am, Corporal, an I can't stand it. I once 'ad a mommy, boy, and she was good. Every once ’en a while she sung this piece. Look at me! I got mixed up inta trouble an now I 'ev ta rot 'ere!" "Yes, I understand. 

I have a favorite song, too, a song sung to me in my baby days." "W'at was it, Tait?" "I-I'd rather not tell." "Ya mean?" "I-I'm here, John, and I-I want to forget.' "Your song, Tait." "They're talkin' ta ya, Tait." 99 "Oh, my song, it's sad. A song which makes one think of home, a nice fireside, a good mother; but my mother's gone. She's dead." "But the song?" ""Nellie Gray' boys, just 'Nellie Gray'." "Yer mother sung that?" "Yes, John, she sang it when I was a baby." "Come on, strike up, fellas. This song for the Corporal."

The card game had progressed in great fashion. Each was winning and losing his share. An hour had passed. 

It was well toward ten, when the men became aware that Top-Sergeant O'Leary had arrived, by the rap on the barrack door. "Hey, yez idiots, time fer bed." "The Sergeant, men." "Open up, I say, open up!" "Go ahead, open the door, 'Spider'." "I warned ya sap-heads not to play too long and now

53 

SANDS 

yez stay here disobeyin' orders. Git ta bed or I'll throw yez in the guard house."

The night had prospects of being a pleasant one, but the entrance of O'Leary spoiled all. Slowly making their way to the bunks, the men began tearing off their coats and shirts, and letting them lie where they fell. Barrack "A" was wrapped in slumber. Only an occasional turn of some tired body gave evidence of the gripping heat. 

Morning dawned-the time of peace. The sun rose slowly on the Taldemalt Plateau. The bugler of Krim had arisen early. As the last echoes of reveille were sounded, these derelicts of the desert seemed to rise as one, fasten their clothes and leggings; then march out on the parade ground. After a few setting-up exercises, they broke ranks and rushed into the mess room. 

After the morning meal was over, women were the next things these soldiers turned to. The Berber women had arrived early on this morn. Much earlier than usual. 

Small tarnished pieces of femininity wrapped up in packages and dealt around to the men for playthings. Their garb was fantastic, highly colored with long, flimsy flowing skirts, a small jacket-like affair which partly concealed their bosoms, and topped off with a burnoose covered with spangles. Many of these women could have chosen desert chieftains for their husbands. 

Did they prefer the men at Krim, or were they only playing?

On the far side of Krim was the veteran company ready to leave for civilization. Major Le Brande, the officer in command of this outfit, seemed changed. His actions had altered within the last few days, his face had taken on a happy, buoyant aspect. The members of the company, too, were eager. Civilization was dear to them; they had cherished its memory for years. Some would reenlist, others would try to regain a place in the world. "Avantè marche," came a thrilling command. The men swung into step and out of the gates of Krim.

CHAPTER V 

Now Krim could quiet down, since these old men had left. 

New légionnaires remained in their place, raw recruits to man this veteran fort, pace its balustrades and entertain the painted dolls of the desert. Painted dolls, who in time would fade, whose faces would coarsen from the use of cosmetics; yet they pleased these soldiers. Their sensuous dances were much enjoyed. Their oriental ways were something new. Here they were, gathered around the fortress, as the companies were dismissed. Again the hardened voice of O'Leary broke forth. "Hey, ya idiots. Don't these women pep ya up? They're lov'ble creatures!" "Did you hear that rotten deal, John? He expects the men to entertain these miserable women.' 99 "The Sergeant knows best, Corporal Tait, 'sides they're not so bad lookin'. Maybe it's the best thing ta do." "Not for me. They grate on my nerves." "Yer goin' off yer 'ead agin, buddy. Ya jest 'ave ta do something in this climate. These women, they're beautiful, they like us an what more kin we ask?" "I know, John, but doesn't your manhood mean anything to you? Doesn't your mother- "Mine's dead, Tait." -?" All trails had their beginning at Krim. Here were trails which led over the sand hills, toward the several desert chieftains' camps; others led on to civilization! But Lola and her lover were to follow the sand beaten path around the fortress. Perhaps she didn't care to lead John over the tracks to her camp. They were to have one pleasant hour together. An hour of love! John was entranced with this slender woman, whose height was scarcely more than five feet three inches. His eyes roved over her divine figure! He noted her careless grace, her sensuous dress!

Her olive skin shone and her face was radiant with the eagerness of a young girl. John wondered if he had created as fantastic a picture in her eye. He would have liked that. Slowly they moved, occasionally talking, more often dreaming. Now they stopped, a sudden halt, for a shrill trumpet blast pierced their ears. John turned to the girl and spoke. "Lola-Lola, ya 'ear that! Maybe I 'ave ta go back ta drill." "Not your bugler, my man, already you forget the sound of your trumpeteer. They are the trumpets from my camp. 99 "Your camp! And where do you live?" he remarked in tender tones. "A mighty camp, my dear one. A mighty camp over yonder."

"What's its name?" "Camp Kirzah-you not hear of ze Camp Kirzah?” "No; only 'ere a few days an 'ev not 'ad time to look around." "Oh, no-no, you must nevaire come there. Perhaps he was calling one of the dancing girls before heem when that horn blew." "Dancing girls ?” "Yes-like thees, there you see, we shake here, zen a leetle there." “Ah-a-a, interesting, but you dance in the fortress?” "Sometimes when zey have guests, ze high man, he sends for us, zen we come and dance." "What you say, John?" “I—a, oh—a, nothing, nothing."

For a time they walked in silence. The sand was blowing and swishing up against the sides of the balustrade. 

The air was foul with the desert stench. Finally John broke the spell with: "Lola-ya lead me around and I'm so tired.” "I theenk you beeg and strong." "Well, I am, but sometimes I feel tired." "I like walking wiz you."

DEATH SANDS 

59 Again they were gripped in silence. Why on earth had 

Lola suggested this walk around Krim? Perhaps it was her desire to be alone with this légionnaire. Possibly she was filled with a love desire. Greedy looks crept out of their eyes, then they turned in surprise to meet each other, as their lips mingled in a lingering kiss. Lola was the first to recover. "Oh, my John, he is so wonderful. Thees ees verie great fort. Theenk of many times it changed hands. Now eet belongs to French, maybe later to Arabs. Don't you theenk so?" "Rave on, desert lily, my ears are open." "You must not talk thees way. I not like eet.” "Dam'me, Lola, all I know is this sand is creepin' inta my leggins. It 'urts, I tell ya, 'urts, and oh-oh-oh-ah, darn it, jest for a woman." "Poor John, your Lola sorry. She understand. She take your shoe off zen rub ze foot." "'Ere, unfasten the leggin'." "Yes, it get bettair soon." "Yer rather awkward about takin' off leggin's." "But I can try." "Right-o." "I like ze French soldair, zat ees why I do thees for heem." "You're all right, baby! Maybe my friend, Tait, 'e's an educated chap, ya know, maybe 'e'd say ya 'ad perfect etiquette. 'E always uses 'igh falutin' words. Makes the other fellas mad, but I like 'im. 'E's probably playin' cards now. Worries me 'bout that kid, 'e don't like girls." "That ees nice theeng to say, John. Your friend, Tait, he must be gentleman. Maybe he have had much hurt.

"Your poor friend, I wondair eef he be so nice een five years. He theenk ze ladies are bad, but you like zem." "Ya, and you, why yer the sweetest one of 'em all." "You like excitement, soldair. Maybe exciting theengs happen when you here. Maybe soon." "Don't make me laugh, woman, excitement 'ere would be a joke. The companies before us never 'ad any action. 

It only takes a handful to govern this desert waste. We only 'ave five companies 'ere." "You plenty smart, zen maybe you can tell why ze French General Office sends more men zan usual to watch over thees desert of ours." "The natives seem afraid of us, though I don't think we'll 'ave any trouble." "You theenk not?" "Our men surrender to Arab tribes? N-E-V-E-R! It's not done in the Legion. We fight to the death." 

Lola laughed softly. "You men say such funny theengs, but I like you much so now you can keess me again." "If ya can stand punishment, I can." "Zat nice keess, eet makes me sheevair." "They all shiver when I kiss 'em.” "Then you use to keess other women?" "One or two." "An I thought, I only woman you ever loved like zat.” "You are. 99 "Nice soldair boy." "Ya-a-a, nice girl, too."

They walked quietly now, exchanging an occasional caress. Both were occupied with their own thoughts. Lola was interested in the gray walls of Krim. She kept pressing against the bricks now and again, she might discover a weakness somewhere, but she was careful not to show too much curiosity. John, on the other hand, had been thinking of all that had transpired during their walk and suddenly he grew embarrassed. "Say, Lola?" whispered John, turning toward her.

A loose stone had aroused the native girl's curiosity. A curiosity which got the better of her. As John turned aside, she hastily pressed the stone and a large cavern revealed itself. Giving the stone a quick kick the passageway was hidden almost as soon as it had been revealed. 

This was worth remembering. John had been too interested in the whirling sands to notice any appreciable change on the girl's face. "Oh, John, please rub Lola's foot-eet hurt so." A harsh, coarse yell leaped from O'Leary's throat. "Hey, ya wimmin folks, git out a here and be quick about it. We seen enough of yez. Clear out, I say, git goin'. Don't stand there gappin' as if ye've niver heard me talk before."

DEATH SANDS 

63 One command from the Sergeant was enough. These desert daughters were angry, as they filed away in disgust. 

In the beginning he had treated them kindly; now they were made to feel as worms under his feet. O'Leary really liked these women, but he had received orders to rid the fort of women during drill time. As these jewelled lilies retired, they mocked O'Leary with different signs of indignation; while his fellow légionnaires looked on in glee. 

O'Leary knew he was hated in the Legion, but he cared not. Years before he had distinguished himself in a skirmish, though the incident was like a forgotten song in the General's office. The Sergeant could never rise to a higher rank, try as he would, for he made more enemies than friends. Regardless of his brutal nature, it was known he would sacrifice everything in a battle to save his men. 

Yes, a true Legion officer and well deserving of a lot more credit than he was given. Such thoughts were running through his mind as on they drilled. 

The mighty monarch of the blue skies was high on his throne. His messengers, the sun rays, threw off intense ribbons of heat. Every now and then, one of these ribbons would catch a fellow in his eye and cause him to teeter on his feet. Then, loosening his grip on the musket, he would drop in a dead faint. Another command, "Hey, there young fella, grab that man over there. Git him out a here quick. Take him ta the hospital. It's a fool who'd fall in ranks. Yer all saps, Ha-ha-ha! Take him away, be quick about it." "The man's weak, Sergeant. You can't rush him.” "Yer jabberin' again, Tait. drill yez, the less sense ya git. yez lunkheads need." "Yes, sir."

"Guess that cooks me, Tait," said John aside. "Don't let him get your goat, buddy! Laugh it off!

Yer goin' to stand this way for a spell. See how long yez kin stand there without movin' a muscle. Yez know, it's a good lesson in selfcontrol."

Each man stood silent until the Sergeant found time to change the order of things. One-two hours went by and the drill was over.

CHAPTER VI 

IT WAS eventide in Camp Kirzah. A truly remarkable place suggestive of the Orient, and the bright silver-like moon on this eve had just begun to project his head from behind a blanket of clouds. Nothing, as yet, had happened in this impregnable stronghold of the desert monarch, Ali Ben Kirzah. Sheik Ali, as he was known among these desert people, was their leader, their protector, and the interpreter of the laws of Allah. It was to him that his tribesmen owed much; it was his crafty brain which kept their heads above the desert sands. It was the same brain which made his people the chief caravan carriers of the great desert. All recognized his authority save Sheik 

El Kabadahl. All bowed willingly before his commands. 

A Monarch indeed!

His camp was the beauty spot of the tribes. It was his culture that made his camp such. A desire for charm that made him surround himself with inspirational Berber girls. 

Berber women who, on this eve, were bedecked in their finery; so that they might be enjoyed by the slow roving eyes of this crafty Kirzah. A glimmer of campfire lights, a silver path from the mighty moon shone down and uncovered all. The bright orange and black striped tents of Kirzah were colorful. An orange and black which stood out in contrast to the surroundings. Campfires, an occasional pot brimming over with some stew. Perhaps a roast chicken was to be on the menu in the next tent. All seemed to be preparing for a feast in this sand-carpeted village of tents. Some alluring enchantress of the desert had caused a feast night. The wide expanse of sand had taken on the freshness of a seaside resort. The fleecy white clouds were dancing lightly across the moon.

All 65

DEATH SANDS 

An was ready, even the sky with its millions of little lights seemed to beckon the gayly bedecked natives on and on. 

The night was fitting! Enchantment and supernatural things seemed to govern these people. But why shouldn't they believe in supernatural beings? Only three or four in the Kirzah tribe had taken the advantage of an education. Something rare among sons and daughters of desert tribes; so anything that detoured from the natural order of things was explained by some myth near at hand. Kirzah's only way to keep them under his thumb was to tell them stories. Furthermore, he had no desire to lose his worldly possessions; for a wealthy caravan trade was the source of his money. He had received an education. Perhaps the best a desert chieftain could give his son. education at Oxford University, and it too had its advantages in governing these people. For ages this tribe of Kirzah had flourished; his family it seems held the reigns of fire over these people. Hence, the leadership of the clan had naturally become his heritage. In the matter of caravan carrying much has been said. Kirzah had grown rich by becoming the truck horse of the desert, was a dealer in dates, and a wise horse trader. His people had become wealthy and alone had learned to regard the French soldiers as mere puppets in their hands. To the members of Kirzah's tribe, these soldiers were supposed to be on friendly terms with them. They in turn were told to suffer the friendship of these légionnaires for the time being and maybe some gain would come from it. 

In regard to caravan carrying, that was not all. A mistaken idea had been gathered by many. Where did Kirzah get his money? How was it possible for a man to accumulate so much money in trading? Yes, a trader; but the mighty Sheik's money was tarnished greatly with blood. 

His fortune, it seems, had been gathered together by robbery and thievery. A thieving tribe engaged in all the criminal arts known to mankind. Perhaps this was the reason the French had shifted so many men to the Fortress

DEATH SANDS 

67 Krim. Perhaps, too, they were determined to hunt down this culprit of the desert. But Morocco is a large country and the sands of death bury many. However, we will not fathom the reasons of the French soldiers for being here now, but will look again at Sheik Ali's tribe. 

It was his boast that none could best his Tuaregs in the art of horsemanship. So, wouldn't it be easy for them to steal upon some innocent bystander, rob him, and leave him at the mercy of the desert. That was the trick!

Kirzah's men were busy creeping upon caravans at night, robbing them, then a quick jump upon their horses and off as mysteriously as they came. The French Government knew that the culprit operating in this territory had the brain of a genius. Such were the facts! The next trick was to catch the man open-handed. Only the stars up in the heavens seemed to be the innocent onlookers of the schemes by which this Monarch worked. If they could talk, perhaps they would tell gruesome stories about the many overdue desert traders. Moroccan traders whose bones rotted on the desert sands of the Northern Sahara.

Robberies committed by the ruthless Kirzah and his tribe of marauders. Such then was the tribe. The tribe that 

Lola and these other Berber women belonged to. 

A feast night was in progress. Natives who bowed to the will of Allah, the omnipotent, were engaged in making preparations to the most frenzied order of things. Kirzah, their leader, their protector, had emerged from his tent and was standing well toward the center of the group. 

His hawk-like eyes watched all proceedings in a greedy fashion. A man ready to drop upon his prey! On this eve, Kirzah was restless; now pacing back and forth, now sitting cross legged on his favorite cushion. Something was crowding his brain. He was thinking, thinking deeply as he drew his fingers through the sand. Then he glanced up in savage glee and in a sharp nervous voice called to his Lieutenant. "Ben Neubal, Ben Neubal! Neubal, it seems, was one of the few educated men around the vicinity of this camp. For this reason, Kirzah kept him under his charge and instructed him in the arts of cunning. 

Ben Neubal was everything that one could look for in a spy, a man with a sharp analytical mind, a dark swarthy complexion, smooth shaven, and actions truly befitting an oriental. His clothes, which were of European make, seemed too large for him. Neubal was rapidly approaching Lola's tent. Opening the flaps of her desert tent he softly called, "Lola-Lola!" Lola, the desert Monarch's ward, slipped out of her tent. More like a mouse shrinking away from some ferocious animal, than like a human being. She seemed afraid; yet she must face him. His word was law, and to disobey him was like pouring oil on the fire. She was trembling as she quietly tiptoed to where her Master was sitting.

Kirzah's face seemed to envelop itself in a frown. The beginning of a thunder cloud was sweeping over his countenance. Lola approached the center of the group. A bright fire illuminated the faces of all. A dance was to begin, a dance with as many warm glows as the fire itself. The wild music of the Berbers commenced. The low resounding beating of the tom-toms and the few sharp notes struck on that barbaric instrument the flageolet sent writhing movements of the dance through the frames of these women. Here was Lola, the leader of these girls dancing, dancing to please the mighty Ali Ben Kirzah. A slow tempo had started the music, but soon it had gradually increased in speed and fervor. A speed which sent them swaying from side to side. Yet, it was not enough; the music hadn't keyed them to a high enough pitch. Quickly throwing aside their burnooses, the women abandoned themselves to the spirit of the dance. Joy passed over the faces of Kirzah and the male members of the tribe. The dancers were satisfying them; and they acknowledged it with smiles. On-on the dance continued; it seemed to be approaching an endurance test. Kirzah was pleased. 

Again Lola was excelling them all as the star dancer of the tribe. Soon she would drop, then he would go in, pick her up and carry her back to where he had been watching the feast dance. CHAPTER VII 

WITH Lola in his arms, Kirzah moved into the brilliantly lighted tent. A tent remarkable for its many beauties; costly oriental rugs adorned the floor, torch-like candles stood at convenient places and rare incense burners adorned the teakwood tables. A jeweled chandelier, hanging from the center pole, gleamed with its many flickering lights. Fine old pieces of tapestry added their beauty to odd pieces of furniture inlaid with mother-of-pearl. This desert chieftain had an over-educated sense of beauty. 

Perhaps it was the barbaric side of his nature keeping pace with the occidental side. Even his jeweled swords made one's mind drift back to the realms of the orient. The many pillows and ottomans, placed here and there upon the floor, were suggestive of his indolent moments. Crossing the tent, he gently placed Lola among the pillows; then hastened toward one of his jeweled tables. It was a cigarette he needed. A short smoke to calm his nerves. For the first time in his life Ali Ben Kirzah had fallen in love. 

Like all good Mohammedan chiefs he had selected the fairest daughter of his tribe. There was a rustle in the next tent, and a little fat head peered in to say, "Oh, Master, Master!" "What do you want now?" "The feast, it there on table. You ready to eat?” "Not yet, I wait; but bring some wine.” "Yes." "I must rouse this girl." "She sick? Maybe she fool you." "Move! Get the wine! I'm not asking your advice." 

Lola, the Berber girl, was asleep. Her guardian bent 75

over her, wrung her hands and tried his best to awaken her, but it was of no use. She was exhausted. "Master, the wine. There not much in the flask." "Come, not all day, this girl fell exhausted in the dance." "Then she carried by you?" "That's none of your affair."

Kirzah leaned forward suddenly. His countenance was black with anger! "Then why does my henchman at Krim send information? He reports plans coming through. Come, what do you say!" "I'm sorry, nothing."

Lola had lied and Kirzah knew. Her deceit had worked him to a frenzy. Jumping to his feet he seized and dragged her over to a blood-stained post.

Kirzah was naturally cruel, and while he was fond of the beautiful, yet he delighted in this gruesome bloodstained whipping post. It was his friend. It brought about the things that his suave manner and persuasive tongue failed to accomplish. Here he tied Lola while he

DEATH SANDS 

laughed long and boisterously as a new amusement was about to commence. "Come, make quick work of it!" snarled Kirzah as two men approached through the tent entry. "You're not whipping your ward, Kirzah?” "That's my affair! Fasten her arms to the post! Quick! 

Tie her feet!" "Please be kind, Master." "Pig! You ask me to be kind when she holds back plans sacred to Allah? Bring me the strap! Get out of my way!"

Purplish red marks, stinging welts of the lash made their appearance upon the creamy white back of Lola.

Blisters filled with water lined the livid welts where the lash had fallen. A pain which sent the girl into rounds of convulsive crying-distorted features, eyes turned toward the east as though she might be begging Allah to end this torture. But to Kirzah this was a conquering moment. 

His mind had reached the limits of excitable frenzy. As each stroke found its mark he grinned maliciously. With easy movement, Kirzah drew back and applied the lash with all his fury. She was being punished for her disobedience. A white froth was upon his thick lips. He was mad! His frame shook with maniacal laughter, while at his side his servant was begging. "Ha-ha-ha-ha-ha-ha!" "Please, Master, you're hurting the girl!" "Give heed to your own affairs." "But she'll be sick, Master." "My strength-is leaving me. It's almost gone.' "Allah be praised!" "Bring me wine! Here-here-another glass!" "You're drinking too much, Master." 99 "One needs a stimulant, after such exertion. Unfasten the girl! Take her to the pillows yonder!"

In a gentle manner Abdul, the head servant, approached Lola. He desired to be kind and in a way atone for the

DEATH SANDS 

83 cruelty of Kirzah. Slowly, but deftly, he released her hands, unbound her feet. Lifting her in his arms, as a father might, he carried her to the pillows. Filling his hands with oil, he softly massaged her aching body. Lola groaned and writhed in pain. Kirzah sat silently by and observed, from lowered lids, the ministrations of his servant. His thoughts were mingled with shame for himself and displeasure for Abdul. The sly cunning of the oriental mind is hard to fathom! With a smile on his lips and accursed hate in his heart he spoke. "Bring me a cigarette,

Abdul!" Kirzah's hand shook as he reached for a match. 

He continued, "You're a very good nurse, Abdul. Perhaps you can remove the scars. You're brave in the presence of your Master, too! Perhaps, some day, I may have a mission for you to perform!"

With groans and sighs Lola tossed and turned, as Abdul rubbed her back as softly as the touch of a feather. 

She was grateful for his tenderness; but to Kirzah the work of Abdul was amusing. He helped himself to an apple. When half through eating it, he remarked, "You missed your calling, Abdul. Instead of a servant, you should have been a doctor."

Two lips met in a kiss-a soft utterance by Kirzah then, "Good night, dear, Allah will guide your health."

Lola, the desert jewel, the supreme daughter of this tribe, had left the tent of Sheik Ali Ben Kirzah. Just a few steps from the master chieftain's watchful eye, and she would be at home,-in bed. She ran in all haste, for she was anxious to retire.

These females of the tribe were ordinarily the working bees, but not so with Lola. She was pampered as an only child. It was she who needed to keep her form, to be ready, and keep on the alert for work at Fort Krim. She knew this desert chieftain admired her, but she couldn't quite bring herself to endure his many embraces and kisses. The evening had been terrifying! First he had beaten her, then he had made stupid love until she had grown weary of his advances. She dared not slap his face; he wouldn't have understood. She hesitated to insult him; since it might result in another beating. What could a defenseless woman do to protect herself against the forced attentions of such a man? Pretend to give in? Exactly!
