CHAPTER VIII 

ANOTHER morning, Camp Kirzah awakened to one of those sweet cool breezes which sometimes blow across the desert plains in the last morning hours. A cool breeze which exhilarated one and made one feel ready for the ordeal of heat which was to follow during the day. Lola was awake. To her the morning seemed like an aftermath of an all-night party. She was weak, her back ached, and the punishment she had received in Kirzah's tent depressed her spirits. The sun had lifted his face above the Taldemalt Plateau. Had bid all good morning and was ready for his duties of the day. Early morning risers of the camp began to follow the quaint old custom of their ancestors, a prayer to the merciful God Allah. Lola was sleepy. The feast alone had been too much for her, regardless of the trying ordeal in Kirzah's tent. At the first peep of dawn Lola tossed in her bed and began to open her dreary eyes. A bed was strange in this country, but Lola had tasted of civilization and wanted the conveniences which civilized women enjoyed. It afforded her many comfortable moments. Yvette hadn't been able to shake Lola's love affairs from her mind. So when her mistress awoke she plied her with a barrage of questions. "Miss Lola, already it is ver-rie late and the Master will be angry with you." "Oh-ho-hum! I seem to crave sleep, Yvette, guess I'm lazy. He won't harm me." "You never know what he'll do." "Back to Krim this morn, I suppose, Yvette. Just the same old work. Ho-hum!" "You're going back again?” "Yes."

90

DEATH SANDS 

91 "And why?" "I don't know.” 

Lucky for Lola that she was cautions with her maid.

She had not told Yvette anything of her true mission to Krim. Yvette was a devout French woman and for some time had tried to loosen Lola's tongue; but the desert jewel ran true to her race-she knew nothing. Yvette was the true French type, she was adept in the use of cosmetics; probably the very thing which made her appear younger than Lola. Then again it may have been because she had been raised in a temperate climate, while Lola had been nurtured in the desert. Lola felt at home in this country. It seemed a part of her; even as the fancy dresses she had hanging in her tent. In a way, she had profited by her contact with civilization; but even though she had many continental garments, still, like the American Indian, she reverted to her tribal customs regarding her attire. "Yvette-Yvette." "Yes, Mistress." "This place makes me have a wanderlust at times. I want to travel about-see things. Guess I've seen too much of civilization, dear." "So soon, you wish to leave?" "If only we had enough water here. The Master commands us to use only the smallest amount. I miss the baths." "I miss my France, the Parisian boulevard. Easter morning with its showy parade of lovely clothes, then a service in the Cathedral of Notre Dame. Here, it's a wonder we can ever wash. No rain for months." "Nothing but sandstorms, Yvette, and we can't wash in them." "Indeed we can't! The Master, I suppose, wants you clothed nicely on this day." "Yes, bring the burnoose, the jeweled one, and that bright colored kerchief. You know, if it's Krim this morn, everything must look its best."

The lone morning hours in Camp Kirzah slipped byone by one. Lola, the beloved ward of Sheik Ali Ben 

Kirzah, had departed. In the tent entrance, Kirzah stood watching the receding forms of these women. He was waving a silk handkerchief fashioned in an odd design. It was wonderful to him. The sight of these women going on a mission to Krim, each bedecked in wonderfully brilliant clothes. Each, it seemed, had tried to excel the other in dress. Playthings these women dubbed the légionnaires.

Toys to amuse them until they were tired, then to be broken and cast aside. French légionnaires matched against Berbers. These men, the bravest known, had sold their lives for a half-penny a day. By using women, Kirzah aimed to break the morale of the French troops. Vile aspirations for such a man as this insolent Sheik, but one could not expect better of him. It was the most efficient way he knew. Hastily glancing up both sides of the main thoroughfare he uttered inarticulate words, and laughed loud and long. "Ha-ha-huh! A Kirzah, my Allah! Yesyes, it should be! Ha-ha-ha-ha!" "Here here, Master, come in. You'll invoke the wrath of your people. Please please stop the laughter." "Ha-ha-ha! I-I, did anyone ask you to speak, pig?”

DEATH SANDS 

"No." "Then close your lips." "The wine, Master." "Yes, you know, Abdul, you're not such a bad servant. 

I don't know, perhaps you wouldn't make good cannon fodder. Ha-ha-huh! But you love Allah?" "Why-why, yes, Master, and why not?" "Nothing. Ha-ha-ha! Oh-oh, Allah, if-if only I could stop this laughter!" "You've seen a doctor, Master?" "I've seen them all, Abdul." "What did they say, oh merciful Master?" "Why do you want to know?" "So I might help you." "The doctors all say, it's-it's uncontrollable, that it can't be helped." "There's nothing to be done, then ?" "Nothing, Abdul, the spells are liable to come on any minute. I just laugh and laugh. It-it's great, Abdul, but sometimes I wish I were dead; for people whom I meet might think me crazy. I'm not, though, Abdul. No! 

Ha-ha-huh-huh-huh! You're a good servant, some day you'll perform a great service to Allah!" "I always hope to please him." "It can be arranged, Abdul." "How ?" "I'll fix it. Why I-I'm his interpreter for the tribe here!" "Yes, Master." "Don't be too anxious." "Of course not, Master." "He he's destined you for a great mission." "A great one?" "Yes." "What kind?” "He doesn't wish you to know as yet." "Maybe my name will be known among many tribes."

DEATH SANDS 

97 "Among all of them, Abdul.” "Thank you, Master."

Kirzah gesticulated, "Help me over to those cushions. 

I'm going to rest until they get back here again." "Yes, Master." "And you may leave, Abdul."

The scene had shifted. Another splendor had come into view. Fortress Krim might well be called a majestic splendor spread on a desert waste, but to some it would appear to be just a little old dried-up French fort-Krim the mysterious-Krim the unusual kept vigil over this vast expanse. A fort again greeted the eyes of the desert daughters as they slowly made their way through the hot burning sands. A barrier gate-all that shut civilization from barbarism. In they marched. Fine clothes, some bedecked in pearls and silks. Never had these French soldiers seen the like before. A haze shadowed their eyes as desert women vamped them with their languorous eyes.

Kirzah had selected the fairest daughters of his tribe to make this journey. A voice more musical than the rest reverberated from the Berber group. It was Lola, the fair one, employing the speech of the Berber women. She approached the sentry, "Oh, mistair man, my French soldair, John, he here?" "We're all here, fair jewel. Ya want ta see him, eh? 

The fine fella of Krim?" "Sure you flatter me, young soldair. But maybe you too like the women?” "To be sure! I'm gay an mighty glad to be a soldier in these parts." "The soldair men. They're lonely?" "Some of them. The men haven't come out a the barracks yet. Ya always git here early ta see 'em." "They're coming now!" "Ya-enter.”

Sergeant O'Leary, the gruff one of the Legion, had found time to speak. "Move on, yez saps. I'll drill yez

Marching to the officers' camp was a thorn in the flesh to these four lègionnaires under arrest. Four men who had broken camp regulations. On to La Tour's quarters they marched surrounded by the arresting patrol. "Come in, Tait! I see you bring the unruly ones." "Yes, Colonel. The men are here." "Show them in, Corporal, station your patrol around the room. It won't take long to dispose of them. Their trial will be over in a few days." "Court-martial?” "That remains. DEATH SANDS 

"Yes, that is the fact." "You men know the penalty for gambling, the punishment, and you make no denial of the charge? Come, let's have an answer." "We make no denial." "Take them to the guardhouse, Corporal, to wait there until their trial."

Slovenly steps retreated backwards. Tait's patrol surrounded them. Tait's patrol had earned the nickname of "The Patrol of Fearless Devils." The Corporal of this group seemed little concerned about the name. He was here to soldier. He cared little about the whole matter, taking these men to the guardhouse didn't interest him. 

His patrol wanted to fight, and hoped their desires would soon be gratified. Across the parade ground of Krim to the guardhouse marched the prisoners to rest until the court-martial. "Here, you fellows, move in here quick!" ordered Corporal Tait in an icy voice. "In here?" "Yes!"

The guardhouse of Krim wasn't much in the way of a prison. Just a small dirty room with bars over the windows and a few wooden beds in it. A room under one of the barracks, thick with cobwebs. Only a few rays of light penetrated the dingy black prison hole. The entrance to the room was gained by a low iron door. These men were not the only guests of the guardhouse. Many rodents with luminous eyes and gnashing teeth scampered about the room. It would be hard to sleep, when the rats began their hungry gnawing. "Come come on, John, this routine is through." "A little excitement, chappie, but somehow I yearn for more." "So did I-an awful hole for these poor devils." "The soldier, he thinks so," spoke Jacques. "Maybe the fellas, they get more excitement soon!"

DEATH SANDS 

101 "Quiet there! If the rats don't eat them or drive them mad, they'll be lucky." "Hey-one of you fellows guard these men!" "Guard duty, Tait?" asked John. "Yes, the first criminals. But no duty for you yet. Go on over and see your Lola." "Thanks a lot, Tait. I don't care to guard down there." "Don't worry. If we can succeed in keeping out of 

O'Leary's way, we'll keep out of trouble." ""E 'ates us, don't 'e." "You said it!" "I-I'm leavin' ya now, Tait. Goodbye." "Going over to see the girls?" "To see Lola, the darling." "She must like you. Go on. I'm going to rest-possibly to smoke and to forget!"

CHAPTER IX 

RANKS had broken long ago. Men had left. Some went to seek their Berber women, others to find their barracks. 

Bodies had been destroyed in this fort. Minds had snapped -but this was Morocco! Could one expect the highest degree of manhood in these parts? Never! If one lived at all in this desert, either he must live apart from the women or inevitably become degraded. Corporal Tait was alone. 

His was the lot of a single man. He cared for no woman; consequently no woman ever asked about him. Tait's friends were few, for he had resigned himself to a fate which has few comrades; and to one which had sent most of them to a living inferno. Of late, this poor chap's mind was uneasy. His soul seemed warped; he caught himself wondering why he was off in a desert waste. At times curses crept from his mouth. A curse that chastised himself for joining this Legion of forgotten souls. Sadness gripped his heart, women seemed his worst enemy, which only he alone could understand. Slowly he went with lazy steps toward the barrack. Here a possible smoking of his faithful pipe would cause him to forget life's picture.

He reclined on the bed and read, as the smoke lazily curled from his lips. Kipling had become his favorite author. "The Songs of the Indian Wars" seemed to inspire himmake him lose himself in the lives of those book characters-but it was only imaginary and that didn't entertain him long. The fortress library was open to all-but few cared to further increase their store of knowledge. Rest, books and smoking seemed the favorite pastimes here. 

John was different. He cared nothing for books, and much for women. He thrived on their flattery. His thoughts were arrested by the intriguing voice of Lola. 102

105 "Let's get started, Lola."

Another walk was to exhaust the légionnaire's tired feet. 

Another short jaunt, perhaps around the desert fortress. 

They might see new sights, maybe not. John was the idol of this Berber woman. She admired his grit, the beauty of his uniform. Maybe it was the latter that had caught her eye. He was a perfect specimen of manhood and she admired that quality in men. Together they walked. Each silently enjoying the other's company. The day had gleamed forth in a new splendor-silver rays of light beat down on the desert sands, made them sparkle more brilliantly than ever, just as Lola's face sparkled forth with a beauty most pronounced. Lola knew what her charm would do to many men; she had fathomed this man's mind and knew the peculiar influence she exercised over him. 

Wasn't this one of the reasons for suggesting a walk to this soldier? John had held a silent tongue for some minutes. He was busy drinking in the Berber girl's beauty, but soon the speech directed itself to the feast night at 

Camp Kirzah.

A clarion blow of the bugle drew all to ranks. Men half asleep, half sick from the heat, lazily slipped into line. 

The bugle ended the day's resting period. Conditions had changed their order. Dreamy-eyed creatures filed out of their barracks. John's brain was in a whirl. He had dreamed of wonderful things; but never had dreamed so much as on this day. A dream filled full of happiness and joy. Pleasant sensations crept over his body as in fancy he embraced his desert jewel. Drill, though, was different, his face turned green at the idea of it. Dreams intermingled with drill crowded his brain. A trip to the desert -a dance enjoyable to any one. It was something out of the ordinary. To him the stirring of men within Krim seemed meaningless. His eyes had assumed a far-away expression. Rest had been denied him, just like it was to all of the men. They needed their women, they wanted to walk, and pour love into the Berber women's ears. What mattered to them if these desert jewels were not of their

109 race. So much the more fascinating. These subtle daughters meant much. So on-on they dreamed of things which seemed shocking to most people. "Hey ya buzzard," broke in Top Sergeant O'Leary. "Hey ya over there. Ya want ta form ranks or is it the guardhouse?"

A lone soldier passed the barrier gate of Krim. A frame tired and aching from the incessant drill and heat. Yet he dared not flinch. He had bragged and O'Leary allowed none other than himself that privilege. He seized every opportunity to enforce discipline. It was necessary for 

France to keep the men under her thumb. It meant her possessions. John was on his way for a hike, this time across the desolate waste of Taldemalt alone. The Ser-

SANDS 

geant had pointed out the objective, told him the time to be back. So alone with silent thoughts he started for this dreary walk. On he paced, hoping every step would tend to loosen the weight of leaden shoes.

Fortress Krim had resumed the order of drill. As far as John was concerned he was on a drill. In a worse position than the men. The painted dolls of the fortress had departed for the desert camp. It was well toward high noon, the sun had pushed his penetrating rays of light over the desert floor. Caused the million little grains of sand to send forth blinding rays of light. Lola had long since departed from Krim, and now Camp Kirzah was in sight. "Holla!" came a voice from the camp. "It's me, Lola." "The Master desires your presence. Hurry," echoed the voice. "He's been waiting for you." "Yes, tell him I will come to his tent at once."

CHAPTER X 

"MY MASTER wanted me?" spoke Lola as she entered Kirzah's tent. "Yes, little gem, I have something important to ask you this noon." "Very well." "Word has been relayed that the French have taken one of my agents under custody." "You mean- -?" "Nothing to fret about, only this. My agent has gotten into trouble. I've repeatedly asked him to refrain from quarrels with those Christian dogs. He's been caught, gambling!" "The penalty, Kirzah, is—” “Ah, you know. Never mind, I, too, know the penalty.

Haven't I studied their regulations? The disciplinary measures these men have to work under! The trouble is this; if they question that fool too carefully, he's liable to endanger all of us." "You think he'll tell?" "Not that, what I need now is someone to watch that trial. Of course military trials are held behind closed doors, but maybe I might use my influence in getting you through. Lucky it is I'm commissioned in their service! 

I-I wonder if you couldn't try to vamp one of those officers so that you might gain entrance?" “Why don't you try to hear it? The afternoon sun had poured forth with heat rays characteristic of tropical countries. Life had come to a standstill in Camp Kirzah. People had gone to rest, desert palms and vegetation round about seemed to wilt in sleep. 

No wind was blowing across the Taldemalt Plateau to refreshen the life hereabouts. Nothing but heat-a sultry atmosphere that brought forth lazy feelings gripped all. 

Lola, the tribal daughter, had left Kirzah's tent. She, too, it seems, was anxious to sleep away the afternoon hours. She had fallen into the lazy habit like all the other natives of these parts. Since coming back from school, she had felt that a few hours' sleep every afternoon meant so much to her physical make-up. Sol had surely done himself proud on this afternoon. He had about broken the endurance of these people, rain had not even made its appearance for many weeks. A drouth would soon commence if Allah didn't arrange to send rain to these people. 

Lola's steps lagged as she entered her tent. "Good afternoon," returned Yvette. "You had a nice morning at Krim?" "Yes and no, Yvette." "And why not? Perhaps your French soldier didn't kiss you." "Oh, misery-misery, nothing but that! Sometimes I feel sorry for those wretched souls." "They enlisted knowing the difficulties." "Yes, but I've just heard about one poor fellow. One man in particular-Corporal Tait.”

DEATH SANDS

Lola was in a deep sleep. The tremendous heat of 

Morocco had seized her, and bathed her fair body in dewy moisture. The evening's proceedings would come forth in a greater zeal if the desert girl would drift deeper into the enchanted dreams of slumber. It was rest she needed -rest which would free her of all cares and worries. Lola's mind carried enough burdens. Here was intrigue stepping in, two men who vied for her love and admiration! Wasn't that enough to tangle any woman's brain? Yvette had withdrawn to her own quarters for sleep. Time went on, one-two hours slipped quickly o'er the desert sands. The drowsy camp began to stir-wake up from this monotony of heat to greet another feast night! Now the entire tribe had recovered their spirits, now they could taste of the joys of life. Things took on a rosy hue when Lola turned over, fluttered her eyelids and called to Yvette. "Yvette! Yvette!" came a freshened voice. "Yes, my mistress ?"

"You look perfectly gorgeous, Lola. I'll watch you from the tent this eve.' 99 "Do-you'll like me."

The walk across the courtyard to Kirzah's tent seemed short. Much too short to the young desert daughter who was pondering over a very weighty question. For the first time in her life she was deeply puzzled. She was in love with two men. First it was Kirzah, now it was the French soldier John. Which one should she choose? Both had their good points. A serious predicament for a young girl to be in. Two men, one a mighty desert chieftain, the other just one of those légionnaires. On the one hand riches; on the other only love. Both seemed to glory in the thoughts of offering her everything, from the earth itself to the sky. They would do anything. Queer, wasn't it? Men going through the pretense of offering all to a woman! 

Do their promises hold good? Seldom! A woman always

DEATH SANDS 

accepts these heavenly dreams of nonsense. They seem to enjoy the make-believe. Lola was no different from the rest; she listened to both of them and smiled. Like the others she must decide-decide which dream, as it was pictured, was the truest; but this is life! All must run the fate line. Lola, the Berber daughter, must think and act quickly; so analyzing her thoughts, Lola passed into the Master's tent. "Oh, Kirzah-Kirzah," came Lola's sweet voice. "Are you in ?" "Always in—yes, always to you, my jewel.” "You-you're liking me today?" "Very much!" "And you're a lot taken?” "With the desert jewel? Much taken-please give me the chance to make you my queen some day!" "Are you so sure you want me? Wait-give me time!

"Yes, but on coming over here I was thinking about the very question on your mind. I'm a woman and it's my right to choose." "It's the way I educated you, that put such notions into your head." "It wouldn't help me, mighty monarch, only make me worse." 99 Disappointment played on the features of Kirzah. “If -if that's the case, you may go; but remember, Allah help you, if you play with me!" "Thanks-I'm so tired and I know sleep will revive me." "The dance starts early this eve, Lola." "Yes, and I'll dance this night. Oh, how I'll dance!" "My lovely one!"

Several hours had passed. For some time Lola had been listening to the flattery of Kirzah. Then came a chant! 

The Mohammedan iman called upon all good tribesmen to pray. A stately figure in his minaret paced back and forth looking down upon the tribe and uttered passages from the Koran. Each personage within Camp Kirzah seemed to understand the significance of prayer. Possibly more so than many Christian people. Kneeling and bowing they prostrated themselves before the Priest. Songs of the tribe were sung-songs which made every loyal subject proud of the fact that he was a Mohammedan. After these proceedings the feast started. Another evening of weird dancing. Kirzah and his entourage listened, drinking in the loveliness of the dancers. Time passed. Lola was now ready to perform for the Master. As she stepped forward, Lola with her sweet gentle voice shook Kirzah, made him thrill in a pleasant manner. "I'm going to entertain you now, Master!"

DEATH SANDS 

"I pray Allah may bless you, send a speedy relief for your sick headache. Remember-dance well!" "Just for you, dear," Lola sighed as she looked towards the hills. "Yes, dear one!" "Watch, then"

The lithe form of Lola gradually swayed to the heavy beats of the Moroccan drum, and the sharp piercing notes of the flageolette. The girl's body moved into the various weird positions of a contortionist. For an hour this dance lasted; then the music died-the dance was ended! "You-you've won, my gem." "You think so?” "Yes-come-give me one kiss, then you may leave." "Here, then-I'm very tired!"

Kirzah was a man of his word. If he kept Lola, it would have been a disappointment to her; so in order to win her adoration, he permitted her to retire. With a final kiss

Lola went hastily to her tent. Here she feigned sickness for a while, then hastened to prepare herself for the hills.

CHAPTER XI 

A TROUP of dancing girls had grown weary of performing. 

One by one they had left, exhausted and famished. Now the Berber lovers were their interest! To Lola, though, the entertainment was over. This weird dancing suggestive of the Orient was finished. It was boresome enough to go through these rites; since something greater than the dancing held her. This feast was an offering to Allah. 

This thought rang in her savage brain so much, that little by little she became disgusted with it all. With Kirzah, the Orient was his strong fort. His mind had drifted back to the savage. Kirzah and Lola were products of this desert. No matter what cultural knowledge you gave them, the desert must claim them! Now she would sink farther back to the barbaric ways of her ancestors. Again she seemed to be rising to the highest pinnacle. Faith in 

Allah, Kirzah's severe temper the child-like minds of her people seemed to weigh her down. If a dissenting word was so much as whispered by her, Kirzah would tear her limb from limb! He controlled everything in this part of Africa and meant to keep his power. This, then, was the reason Lola remained here. A girl torn between two fires. A yearning for civilization and a fear of Allah!

On this eve the moon was low in the heavens, ideal for lovers. It had just begun to peep over the eastern portal of the Taldemalt Plateau. Even the palm trees which were silhouetted against the inky darkness seemed to prophesy romance. It would be a shame if such a moon should go to waste. A night for love! Lola sensed that a minute lost would probably mean destruction. "Quick, Yvette, my wrap," whispered Lola in terrified tones. 125

DEATH SANDS 

"Yes, Mademoiselle was beautiful and danced devinely!" "Only fair, Yvette, my dear. Sometimes the barbaristic nature of it annoys me, sometimes it amuses me. If—if—.” "Then the desert is making you restless?" "Oh yes! If I had some place to go! At times I long to leave this rotten camp. These men, here, sit, staring at you like so many wild beasts. Perfectly satisfied to watch us dance. The oftener we do our rotten dance, the more greedy they seem." "Some day maybe things will turn out better, little desert mistress." Off to the hills tramped Lola; the favorite desert flower of Camp Kirzah. Something impelled her to visit these hills on this night. Her heart beat fast, her body thrilled!

A secret meeting with her soldier! She was nervouscited-intoxicated with this new experience! It might be unknown to her, she was in love with this young soldier. 

If she were in love, she would not voice it, for this man was a Christian and she-a Mohammedan! So strong a belief had she in Mohammed that to admit love for this man would be sacrilegious. Lola knew, too, the results of speaking too freely. Kirzah was ever on the alert, watching

DEATH SANDS 

127 for opportunities to catch his subjects unawares, and leave them to the mercies of the death angel! She, however, cared little for the Monarch's philosophy. Her education on the continent had gained her the viewpoint of the modern girl. So with mounting thoughts, Lola tripped over these dunes. A five mile walk confronted the girl, but what cared she. She loved adventure and it troubled her not at all to fool this boy! Men like flattery; especially from a woman's lips. Time passed, Lola and 

John met. John the soldier had risked both his life and that of his friend Corporal Tait in order to meet his desert sweetheart! Corporal Tait stood aside scanning the dark sands. He thought John a fool, but for the sake of friendship offered him protection. “Oh, Mistair John," spoke Lola. "You-you've come?

I sometimes wondair eef my soldair man, he'd keep, maybe, his promise and come!" "Surely ya couldn't believe I'd desert ya. Not this chappie, and Tait over there, see-influenced 'im ta come too." Fast moving Tuaregs were beating down upon this clandestine meeting place. Tuaregs-the fiercest fighters in Northern Africa, united as one, were moving toward this group. CHAPTER XII 

FORTRESS KRIM had indeed witnessed many a fine morning.

Such a morning would be right for the many excitable things which were to happen on this day. The surrounding country had taken on a solemn aspect; perhaps this was the result of the deadened life within the fort. The people of the tribes were not alone in their dearth of water; the poor miserable souls confined in the fort jail were thirsty; some had taken refuge in sleep just to forget their craving for a drink. They had been forced by the officers to keep awake the last few nights with the hope that they might break down and commit themselves, but they withstood the ordeal like iron men. Today, their trial was to be called. They had been allowed some rest-interrupted by an occasional kick, slap or the scampering of desert rats. They ceased to wonder as to what would happen next! They would soon know their fate, but not until the wheels of justice began to move. Thoughts of discouragement were theirs. Here was a group of men possibly to be convicted and sent to the Legion prison in France. 

So on this last night they were busy writing lettersconcentrating on other thoughts than sleep. Each one gazing into space-wondering what the morrow held in store for him.

Corporal Tait's patrol had been placed in charge of these men. Perhaps this was the reason why this boy and his buddy had been able to escape to the hills on that memorable night. Corporal Tait's duty was to guard them during the trial. Lucky that young Tait and his buddy succeeded in getting back to Krim without being missed! Visitors had arrived at Krim early,—yes, the 

Berbers, sent possibly for a reason. It was a surprised 139

Things progressed. Lola hastened to find her French soldier, John. In the meantime La Tour, the commandant of Krim, opened the court-martial. It was an early hour for such a trial, but the order of the business for the day was severe. Hence this trial must be gotten rid of as quickly as possible. The prisoners, needless to say, were surrounded by guards. At a large wooden table and well toward the center of the room sat the officers who would seal their fate. On the far wall hung a picture of Napoleon, draped in the French tri-colors. La Tour had just adjusted his spectacles, had fastened his glance on the paper before him, then turned and looked upon the accused men.

CHAPTER XIII 

KIRZAH, the Lord Monarch of these vast desert wastes, had been waiting for just such a moment as this. His ward, Lola, was soon ready to return to her desert home. 

In fact, everything had been arranged for her entrance into this stronghold. Most of the natives were bedecked in their finest apparel. A goodly supply of wine had found its way upon the thickly jeweled tables. His servants seemed overjoyed. For to Kirzah a dire scheme had transpired. He had allowed his subjects to dress for this occasion. Even his servants were ready for all action. This was Kirzah's day-the day he had outwitted the French. 

Even the French Colonel, La Tour, seemed only as a troublesome string around his little finger. A string which would soon be broken. Just now the string was beginning to tighten a little, and was about to tear his flesh; but soon the time would be ripe for Kirzah to yank it loose. 

It was then that his tribe could exert their freedom. Kirzah was anxious, steadily pacing the few feet of space within his tent-wondering if his latest plan would work. 

With quick jerky paces he would stop-stamp the ground, then narrow his eyes and look off into space. No stone must be left unturned. The black, silky moustache which circled the upper lip seemed wild; as he twirled it between his long, narrow fingers. His very make-up suggested the triumph of a returning army. He had ceased his pacing as words uttered in a harsh manner escaped his lips. "Here-here you dog servant. I want a glass of wine!

The blood of France-something that will make me strong!" "Yes, you drink again, Master?” "To the freedom of my tribe, fool servant, and to Allah!" 152

DEATH SANDS 

153 "You wish the best wine?" "Yes, the wine I usually serve my guests, for it seems they, too, must gather strength when they're around the desert Monarch Kirzah." 

"Here it is; may I have just a sip if there's any left?" "You need strength-you, don't you see enough of me every day to make you strong? When I drink I drain my glass well. Pour yourself a drink!" "Thank you, Master." "A slight favor, my servant, you have it coming. May 

Allah bless you in all his ardor." Kirzah's words rang out as he drained the last few dregs of wine from the glass. "Thank you thank you, honorable chieftain." "You should thank Allah for such favors, my lowly one. However, you might perform one great favor for 

Allah. You wanted to do something to win his blessing?" "Yes, and is the time coming when I may?" "My time has come, tribal man. Promise you won't tell a soul of what I am about to reveal?” "Not even to my tribal gem?" "Heavens, no!" "All right, then, Allah's law must be obeyed." "Very well, tonight you shall go, with Lola, Ben Neubal and a third trusted tribesman, to the walls of the Fortress Krim!" "To-to the fortress? It is very dangerous-shot maybe in our backs!" "No, fool-it is that you watch your step. Guard yourselves well. Trust in Allah's good graces and things will come out in fine shape, else something worse than death may greet you." "May Allah save us!" "Lola, my ward, has come upon a secret passageway leading in through the walls of this fortress. Tonight, I want you brave sons and daughter of the prophet to investigate that secret chamber!"

DEATH SANDS

Prophet-but you never mentioned this danger before." "Ha-ha-ha! You're getting a little afraid and don't want to do anything for the Prophet, Mohammed, and the

"This is nice of you-very fine of you.' 99 "Back to your quarters, lest you get cold feet and Allah hesitates about warming them for you." CHAPTER XIV 

WILD thoughts crowded with adventure, charm and devilish schemes traversed Kirzah's brain. This was his night -the night when his agents would move towards the 

Fortress Krim. A night when plans and Mohammedan trickery might prove superior to the Frenchmen's art of wisdom. It seemed as though an electric wire had been fastened about him-a button pushed by some unknown force, and the parties concerned would jump into action. 

In a flash his countenance changed. Now wreaths of smiles encircled his face, his eyes snapped as he thought of the many French soldiers who would eventually bow to his will. 

The Master Mohammedan seemed in all respects alert- -a nervous pacing like an anxious cat urged him on. 

Kirzah was a peculiar subject, a man whom education had warped in all outward aspects. Harsh laws of obedience had been pushed into force through all his camps. It was the same man who expected his subjects to bow to him as they bowed while at prayer. With the zeal of a crazy man he would catch himself, race over to the far corner of his tent, stroke the garments and utter phrases which he hoped Allah would hear and also bless. Yet, "To you, Lola," whispered Kirzah, "to you, the desert jewel: Tonight you wear this, the colors of El Kabadahl. 

Though your allegiance is true to me, it is you whom I love. Allah protect you-I know you will come through this mission with safety."

Kirzah gently took his hands off this garment and passed them to the folds of another. "To you, Ben Neubal, my best secret agent: May you come out alive-may you have the blessing of Allah on this mission; but to you, my good servant: Mighty Al- 162

Lola slept. It was a delightful day,—an ideal day for slumber. Even Yvette had tired of looking at the latest fashion news from Paris. She began to yawn; so, laying the book aside, she decided to rest. Kirzah's camp had taken on a stillness such as prevailed only in tropical countries. The wind had died; the tropical palms had drooped their leaves as if in tranquil slumber. Picture the surroundings inside Lola's tent: The mistress asleep on her divan,-Yvette sprawled over a chair. Time lapsed.

The day seemed even more sultry. Penetrating heat rays delved into the very bodies of these people, and sent them crying in desperation to their tents. Beads of perspiration stood out noticeably on every one. Numerous times, some poor soul would be overcome, possibly driven crazy, yell with all his might until his throat would be choked by a mere gurgle. Again the heat had snapped some one's life. 

A limp form greeted the onlooker. Such, then, were the conditions in this tropical country. Hours passed! Stirring movements of people could be heard passing through the camp, but Lola and the group which was to accompany her on tonight's mission were insensible to conditions in camp. CHAPTER X V 

KIRZAH was waiting for Lola and his two henchmen. As to Abdul, the servant, he had already come back to Kirzah's tent and was ready to do his duty by Allah. The other three were on their way. They marched hastily toward his tent, each occupied with his own thoughts. To 

Lola, the mission meant many things. It appealed to her in a romantic way. She was acting in the capacity of a spy. This was hard enough to do, even though Allah was supreme in her mind. Religion meant everything to her and it was by this means that Kirzah swayed her to his will. If, however, she should come back free from harm, her name would ring from camp to camp, but glory is soon forgotten.

Ben Neubal, the wise one, looked at matters in a somewhat different light. He was lifted to the very heights, with a zeal to attain something. It was a new adventure, another thrill, a chance indeed to strike at the French dogs and possibly learn their plans. With these thoughts stirring in his brain, he trod the few steps to the Sheik's tent, happy in spirit as well as in thought. 

The third tribesman, like Neubal, was also thrilled with the zeal of an ambitious man-an anxiety to do great things. Kirzah, in fact, had done himself proud in choosing this man, for he was the best marksman in the camp. 

A brilliantly illuminated tent met the eyes of the three loyal ones as they entered Kirzah's tent. "Good evening, and you three are here to do my work?" "Yes, Chief. You wanted us to come at the first signs of dusk." "Just so, Neubal. You are three of the bravest in the 170

The four set out, leaving Camp Kirzah silhouetted against a dark Moroccan sky. A sky which seemed to cover all schemes whether good or bad-a sky filled with countless stars met their eyes as they stealthily stole towards Krim. They seemed more like moving spectres than human beings. The desert had even closed its ears to the tread of their feet upon the sand. A fiendish work had been set apart for them to accomplish-another one of Kirzah's wicked deeds. Fate, it seems, was holding the

Moroccans on its side and urging them on. A cool, fresh breeze swept over the Taldemalt Plateau as Lola was found a few paces ahead of her companions. "Miss Lola," called Abdul. The four confederates beside the outer wall marched silently on-on till a stone object within the wall was observed by Lola. "It should-should be here," Lola spoke in a subdued tone. "Yes, see if it moves!" "All right-that's funny. No, it's further on-yes. 

Here-here it is!" "Is everything all right, Jacques ?" "Yes." "We're about ready to enter."

Gently pushing aside a small stone in the fortress wall a secret door was opened. Years had passed since it had been used by the French. Many were the tales it could tell, of the struggle between French and Arabs. As this door slowly gave, and creaked on its rusty hinges, nothing but darkness could be distinguished at first. Gradually one could recognize cobwebs hanging on the ceiling, rotten timbers, and desert rats. A passageway which apparently had been forgotten, but now was to serve its purpose! 

Scampering here and there, rats with phosphorescent eyes would look back at the group. They seemed the only sentinels of this part of the fortress. A blazing light of fire beamed from them, which was frightening enough to behold; but on the valiant subjects of Kirzah pushed. Many the times they wished they could turn their steps backward, but they must move ahead if they were to accomplish their purpose. "It-it's quite dark in here," said Lola. "Quiet, maybe this leads to some important place. Take care, we must use caution.” "Sh! Some one's talking!" "Yes-talking. The French dog soldiers of Krim.

On the four spies of Kirzah crawled. The tunnel abruptly turned and led in another direction to a new place. Voices again resounded through the rafters. This time the talking of officers continued. The evils of too many intoxicants in this clime. Then, they could overrun us easily if we forget ourselves." "Ha, here's ta your health, Colonel. Good night!"

With this the four henchman crawled back through the tunnel, each thinking of the proceedings which had taken place in the room above. "You see, Lola-spies in the desert." "Yes, Neubal, it possibly will mean death." "They never pass me though, little girl. Don't worry!" "I hardly think they will.' "Listen, servant, do you want to do a great favor for

Allah? When we get out you cover up the rear." "Yes, then maybe I'll please Allah!” "Yes-your name sung from camp to camp."

DEATH SANDS 

"In regard to John's talk, you won't tell the Master what we heard, will you, Neubal?" "No, my gem-no. I worship you too much." "Yes, I know. My John is a nice French soldier." "I understand, but the Master never would." "Only a few more rods of this crawling, then we're through, Neubal." "Yes, but the biggest mission then, Miss Lola. Bear up under it." "Jacques will be waiting?" "Watching, too."

The four Berbers were half crawling, half walking toward the east in the direction of Camp Kirzah. Lola, Ben Neubal and the third tribesman were well in the front while Kirzah's servant covered the rear. Important news had been procured; but in a way this was more of a tour of inspection. The main purpose being, to determine the possibility of sending men through here and to where the tunnel might lead. So far the tunnel had been traced to the officers' quarters. It had afforded an avenue of escape when the fortress had been beleaguered by hostile natives. 

Jacques, the sentry, was on the wall, pacing back and forth, stopping-peering off into the dark, then continuing on his way. Every few minutes, he'd stop at this entry way and look down from the top of the wall. At a signal from Neubal, the four left, leaving the servant to cover up the rear. Jacques knew well where to find his man; so, raising his rifle, he fired. Something was to happen and happen quick. The form of Kirzah's servant tightened, then crumpled in a heap on the ground. Jacques had done his duty, executed it with ease, and the Moroccan lay on the ground. Anyway, if caught, he could pawn the body off as some thieving tribesman. What cared he?

Moreover the body wore the colors of El Kabadahl and this alone would relieve Sheik Ali Ben Kirzah of all suspicion. He had triumphed and revenge was sweet!

A new wonder had seized Corporal Tait and his buddy as they stole out into that inky night. Their steps led them a few rods away from the wall; but they were like two hungry lions stalking on towards new prey. With a stealthy pace, they moved on to accomplish their mission; so nothing was too daring for them to conquer. This time they were to pick up a corpse, carry it within the fortress wall. A barking gun might stop them in their paths, but that didn't seem to bother them. An investigation was to be made about the killing of an Arab. Lafon staggered to a chair. He was sad and wished to think. Another victim of the World War had journeyed

West. A journey possibly much more beautiful than any taken in our material lives—a trip into the valley of the unknown. Death had come into this fortress twice in one evening. An evil omen, perhaps a grim messenger which seemed to strike all men alike regardless of commission in this Legion! Lafon was trying to fathom this young fellow's past, but always it was the same old story. A man with his life left behind when he signed for the honor and glory of France.

CHAPTER XVII 

FRANCE's authority in Morocco had been challenged. It was now time for her to act, for like anxious cats the Berbers were sitting back ready to pounce upon the fort. 

La Tour was nervous-he had not finished discussing this killing. He remained in his study, talking on and on about the happening of the day before. "Bridwell, this is a strange thing. The officer in charge of Krim had spoken. His business with his fellow men was over; so one by one they filed out of his quarters. To these men the investigation meant real action, something they had longed for—anything to break the monotony they had suffered in these desert wastes.

CHAPTER XVIII 

LOLA, the toast of the desert, walked gracefully through the Krim courtyard with Captain Lunier. She was pleased to win favor with the French officers and enjoy the freedom of the fortress. Lunier was such an interesting man-a true French officer and every inch a gentleman to the desert ladies. Many gallants had long since passed through this French outpost, but none could compare with this dandy. He was overly courteous to women such as these; for in them he found satisfaction for his vanity. He enjoyed it, when they looked up to him in their demure way and tried to find answers to their questions through him. 

He was not the brusque beast, but a kind French gentleman when with them. Lola felt uplifted. With eyes full of wonderment, and an urge for something just outside her grasp, she exerted herself to talk-a speech, which was almost meek, flowed from her sensuous lips. "Oh, Mistair Capitaine-my soldair-maybe you tell me where he ees ?" "Am I not to keep you in my company, Miss Lola? Am 

I not the man for you? [?” "Oh, no-no-no-you are what zey call much too fine, and my John-oh, he ees so wonderful!" "Your man, Lola, is in the barracks. The morning drill is well over-you'll find him there." "Goodness! What ees thees? Thees poor man here! 

Please, Capitaine, what has happened?" "A trick of these Berbers, Miss. A dead Arab we found outside the gates." "And what you do about eet?” "An investigation." "Oh-I just love investigations, Capitaine." 198

199 "Too much, though, for a lady to understand. It is not the policy of my country to mix women with business!" "And who you theenk deed thees, Capitaine?” "No idea-but we hope to find out." СНАРТER XIX 

THE hours slowly passed by. Lola and John had whiled away a good share of the morning hours together. It was approaching high noon and still no sight of the various tribesmen. The sun was in mid-sky; and the penetrating waves of heat beat upon the sun-baked fortress walls. 

Officers within the outpost knew the results of this heat -a drinking condition which tried men's souls, drove them to despair, and made them wild! A little resentment, and a fight might ensue between the Arab horsemen and the poorly equipped légionnaires. It was poor strategy, indeed, to let these Arabs enter Krim; but how could the officers do otherwise? For precaution the troops were drawn up, in army formation, within the fortress. Notices had been sent to all the mighty chieftains to congregate here on this day. France was to test her power. A decision was to be reached as to who would control the desert- 

France or the Berbers?

Off in the distance tribesmen were advancing towards Krim. First to come was Ali Ben Kirzah and his chief henchmen. Kirzah had come to the fort on a mission. He had expected the French officers to order this investigation; so with fiendish joy he engaged his chief henchman in conversation. "Neubal, you know these French-be quiet when I enter the fort. Be not too outspoken, keep your eyes and ears open." "You will place the blame?” "On El Kabadahl-of course, stupid. It will serve the dog right for signing a protocol with them." "Then the French, Master, will wonder where they stand.” 205

DEATH SANDS 

"Stand is right. Ha-ha! They won't know whether they're standing with their backs against the wall or not." "Spies will be sent out." "Yes, Neubal, you told me of this. I shall expect anything." “Depend on me, Master. My schooling-my keen foresight. I shall be ever on the watch." "Allah-oh, mighty Allah! He will reward you, Neubal -reward us all." "Another point, Ali, this Lola, her maid-I meandon't you think she bears watching?" "Yes-Allah and I shall decide later what to do with her." "She's French.' 99 "So much the better. CHAPTER XX 

ANOTHER visitor was to pass within the portals of Krim. 

This time it was none other than El Kabadahl, the venerable chieftain of tribes engaged in a pastoral life. Kabadahl had heard with his ears the suspicions cast against his people, how the French believed his tribes were the guilty ones, and how their feelings against him were at a high tension. The old chieftain suffered greatly over this outrage, and now he must prove to his friends that the guilt lay elsewhere. Like a mysterious holy man, he had ridden into Krim. For some time he had been in the saddle. Quite a trip for a man as old as he! He was puzzled about how to regain the trust and friendship of the French officers. On account of his advancing years, El 

Kabadahl had not been to the French fortress for some time. The men within Krim had nearly forgotten he was a part of the desert and held reign over three thousand tribesmen. If conditions should become worse, the French would have no one to rely upon save the Sheik Kirzah and his assistance could not be depended upon.

Kabadahl had aged much overnight, his growing childishness was alarming—almost frightening. His head seemed heavy, his eyes bloodshot, and he had not taken his accustomed sleep; so that it was with effort he kept his mind busy trying to figure the details of the affair. 

A man arrayed in his own tribal garments had been shot, and not one of his men missing; yet he was accused of plotting against France. Stroking his bearded chin, he called his chief henchman to his side. "The French say I am guilty of this affair. If only,

Nertzah, I can prove my loyalty to them. Colonel La 

Tour will believe my story?" 216

CHAPTER XXII 

THE natives had left Krim and officers within the fortress were talking amongst themselves. A voice rang out for 

Colonel La Tour. 

"Oh, La Tour-La Tour!" "Yes, Captain, you wish to see me?” "All of the officers do, Colonel. Maybe ya will see us now ?" "Surely, over in the quarters." "Fine, Colonel!"

La Tour was cautious; he had need to be; consequently all meetings between the officers were to take place secretly in his quarters. La Tour hurried to his room. Everything was in readiness. Things must move fast. For a long time he had been pondering whether it was a wise thing to send spies into the desert camps. It was a problem for a Solomon, and he wished to be very sure before he made any definite move.

On the road towards Krim was a spectre moving nearer and nearer to the outpost. At first it seemed to be only a small object, but now it grew larger and larger. A stranger was approaching a stranger and traveling alone. "Captain-Captain," came an excited voice. "Look out on the desert!" "Ya, what's the excitement ?" "An Arab! Here, use this glass!" "To be sure, and coming in this direction." "Maybe some more information on this murder case.' "No, I don't think so-detain him when he arrives." "O. K. Any other orders?" "Na-only call if necessary." 99 235

DEATH SANDS 

A long trip for this beggar Arab; but what cared he? 

For weeks he had been traveling towards Krim and now his destination was near. The high grey walls of the outpost stood out on the desert sands; seemingly beckoning him to enter. Austere looking walls that seemed to challenge any one who should entertain the foolish notion of capturing the fort. He shifted his position frequently, in order to relieve the tense feeling of his body. He had been riding in all haste and any comfort would be greatly welcomed when he reached Krim. Nearer and nearer he approached, until at last he reached the gate and with no little effort dismounted. "Hey! Hey, you! Open up the gate," came his challenging voice. "The Captain says not." "And who may I ask is the Captain?" "Lafon-a fella who knows his men." "Lafon-Lafon. What an odd name yet I seem to have heard it before." CHAPTER XXIII 

"THE ARAB's' sleep had been decidedly restless. He felt the effects of the sultry climate, but he must work regardless of this. On through the day and night he slept, and the morning dawned all too soon. Today he was to start on his trip into the mysteries of Camp Kirzah. It was a sleepy man who rolled over on his bed before arising. Already it was near six A. M. and he must soon be on his way. La Tour had already arisen. Hurriedly dressing, he sought "The Arab's' quarters. "Good morning-are you just about ready?" "Yes, Colonel, packing a few necessities." "You enter Kirzah's camp today, my man." CHAPTER 

XXIV 

DAWN,—that glorious time of day in Morocco, was beginning to break. The air was bracing, and the shadows out upon the sand dunes seemed to strike one breathless with their beauty. The cool morning air was gently blowing through the date palms of the oasis Kirzah. The heavens were clear as "The Arab' drifted down into the camp. He must guide his footsteps carefully; so faking his role, "The Arab' limped into the camp, a weary, bedraggled, despised dealer in spices. "Holla!" came a shrill voice from the camp. Like all Mohammedan dungeons it was a hole in the ground over which a few iron bars were stretched. It was to this place "The Arab' was again led. Kirzah had not finished his work. The lull was only caused by the Master 

Chieftain being too exhausted to carry on. "The Arab' felt this was not the end. How well he knew the brutality of this Chieftain, of his novel ways of punishing, and of

DEATH SANDS 

his fierce temperament. Maybe they would let him rot in the dungeon! It was not hard to imagine Kirzah committing such a crime. As the lid was removed from the hole, a shudder ran the entire length of his frame. He turned to the guard for one sympathetic look. "Must I go in there again?" came the spice dealer's awed voice. "Yes. The Master furnishes the cell. You're the bait. 

Not such bad fare, spice dealer." "None too good, but I will stand it." 99 "We have arranged a guard for both day and night.' "What does he expect to happen? He surely doesn't think it possible for me to get away from this rat-infested hole!" "Of course not, but we take no chances." "Tell your Master I send him my compliments and when the French enter this camp they'll wipe him from the face of the earth!" "The hot sun will about drive you insane; so talk while you're able. Maybe it will help you escape death." "I do not talk. I remember my oath to the Legion. My life belongs to France." "Good day. The new guard will take care of your wants."

CHAPTER XXVII 

A VALIANT Son of the Legion had sallied forth, vowing to expose the traitor who was creating the disturbance in the desert, but he had been swallowed by the tribes. Soon he would be only a memory. It was a worried group of 

French officers which sat back eager to hear from the brave agent who had not as yet sent in a report. Other spies had been accounted for-save "The Arab' who was in Camp Kirzah. Hot winds were blowing-a thick, sticky heat had settled upon the fortress. Perhaps ‘The Arab' needed a few more days before he could report, the hot dry winds may have slowed his action. This was reason enough to the légionnaires. He probably suffered the same hardships as they-parched lips, burning eyes and the continued thirst. The afternoon drill had passed. The officers of the old weather-beaten fort had ceased operations for an afternoon chat and siesta. Commandant La 

Tour had just settled back in a huge chair and was trying to be comfortable. Putting his wine glass on the table, he threw his legs across a chair, and looking over his shellrimmed glasses, spoke. "No news from "The Arab', Captain Lunier?” "None, as yet, Colonel. I'm wondering if he's forgotten to communicate with us." "No-no, you're wrong, Lunier. The actions of these 

Arabs are queer." "Your chief spy is an Arab." "I know, all the more reason for doubting. I am not certain that I can trust him. These Arabs have a religion that seems to enslave them, and if Kirzah should press a religious duty upon the spice dealer, he might quit our service." 279

DEATH SANDS 

"Religion is often brought to bear upon these natives by the leaders who are wiser than they, Colonel." "I know it well, Lieutenant Bridwell." "However, during the years I have spent in these deserts, Colonel, I have never known a fellow swearing allegiance to the French; I mean an Allah worshipper betraying his oath." "You've mingled with these people, Bridwell?" "Surely, my Colonel, I've mingled with them and have lived their life." "Then you believe this man in Kirzah's camp is true?" "True to his trust? Absolutely, he's true, but certain conditions keep him from communicating with you. Maybe he has been imprisoned." "If he has been detected, the game is up." "No, send another spy. However, I suggest you wait until tomorrow." "Wisely spoken, Bridwell." "I-I'm wondering, Colonel, if we can cope with any dangerous situation which presents itself to us?" "That's what I said.” "You've been down in South Morocco most of your life, I'd think, Colonel." "Yes, Lafon, for some spell, I've been in these parts.

CHAPTER XXVIII 

ANOTHER day had dawned. Today, the French forces were to entertain the American tourists, warm waves of air were blowing in from the Moroccan desert. The troops in the fortress had finished their accustomed drill. La Tour, the 

Commandant, had held general inspection. It was a day when every man tried to look his best. All had turned their steps towards the barracks.

Corporal Tait had picked up his pipe and started drawing a few puffs, while John was in his bunk resting and wiping his face with a large piece of cloth which served as a towel. 66 ""Oly mackerel! What a warm day!" shrieked John. "It is rather warm, isn't it, and here we are stuck off in the worst part of the globe. A nice day at the beach wouldn't be so bad, but not for us, buddy, no-we have to sizzle in the desert." "Yeah, and I'm tellin' ya it's days like this that gets the best of one." СНАРTER XXIX 

It was midday; the sound of bugles, men scurrying here and there, curses and mutterings furnished the atmosphere of the fortress-monotonous drill, a little rest-then more drill. This was the légionnaire's daily fare. Some were in happy moods, others were wrapped in the clothes of melancholy. Yes, it was a happy day for some, for today real honest-to-goodness tourists were coming. For many months, these men had yearned for their own kind of civilization instead of a friendship with the Arabs. Tait, unlike the rest, was filled with apprehension. He hated the thoughts of these people coming. Much more, since they were from his own country, and it recalled unpleasant memories. He had just returned from La Tour's quarters. His reactions were reflected by his dejected spirits. “What say ya, chappie?" voiced John, half rising from his wooden bed. "Are ya goin' to perform some daring mission?" "Nothing of that kind. No-they-they wanted meif-if I could only forget. Why do they have to find me here ?" "Speak-what's wrong?" "You know, John, you're the only person I've ever talked to around here." "Yeah, I know." "The officers wanted me to show these tourists around the fortress, because I speak English a little better than the rest.' 99 "And you accepted?" "Of course not, I didn't care to. I might be recognized by someone I know, among them. I know it's improbable 295

Eager men hastily formed ranks. Tait's patrol was farthest away and at the end. Suddenly a command rang out. It was the call to attention. Now the guests were to pass in front and review the ranks. La Tour was late in arriving. He was bothered with the thought of tourists visiting Krim and was half provoked as he made his way out of the quarters and towards the guests. This inspection was to occupy some time. There would be talking and some explaining to be made by the officers. Many questions would be asked and answered. During the time consumed, the men must stand patiently by. An American banker, his wife, and their friends were in their midst, and it was to the benefit of the French to display these desert soldiers. 

Foolish tourists-always seeking new adventure, even in this most distant outpost. Officers and men alike were bored. In a half apologetic way La Tour extended his hand to his guests and broke into conversation. "Good afternoon-I believe the Americans wish to examine the fort."

"Yes, Colonel. I think that's the program for the day," returned a distinguished, middle-aged man. "Very well. La Tour's orderly had already taken stock of affairs. 

Nothing in the world would suit him better than to see this young boy court-martialed. Instinctively he hated the Corporal. Barely opening the door he shoved Tait into a mixed crowd of officers and guests. For a while Tait stood in confusion. He had reasons to expect the worst. 

Maybe he was to be put under arrest and ordered to a more dangerous outpost, or spend a term in prison. Fear, however, did not take complete possession, even though he trembled with weakness. His walk was shaky, his face was drawn, and his hands nervously found their way in the lapels of his coat. He had aged-it seemed forty years since he had fallen in line. He seemed to see people and things about the room in a haze. "Corporal Tait," sternly uttered La Tour. "Yes," came a quiet voice. "Today you demeaned yourself in strange fashion, before my guests." "I know, Colonel, and--and I'm sorry. Something happened. I can't explain!" "You disappointed me, Tait. I have considered you one of the bravest men in the Legion. Now you show weakness." As Tait kept on with his work, each one seemed harder to please. After finishing, he might have to stand for a possible beating. The officers were angry, because they couldn't force the lad to tell why he had weakened in ranks, but Tait knew it wouldn't do to tell them; so he worked and took their sarcastic remarks. Things went well until he came to Benton's wife. She was the last to have her shoes shined. As he approached her, he hesitated; it seemed as though he couldn't go on. Suddenly dropping to his knees, he looked off into space and started to work.

DEATH SANDS

Mrs. Benton stood perfectly still. A hot wave passed over her body. For the first time in her life she was ashamed!

CHAPTER XXXI 

TAIT left. His work was finished. He had escaped with a lighter punishment than he expected, but he had been shamefully humiliated. "It's my best vintage, Mrs. Benton." "Let's drink to the health of these brave officers," interrupted Mr. Benton. "You flatter us, Monsieur Benton." "To the health of all-to Colonel La Tour!"

There were shouts of laughter and a clinking of glasses. 

After some two hours of feasting and pleasant visiting the meal was ended. All were having a splendid time, save 

Mrs. Benton! Her spirits drooped-she couldn't forget the shameful incident of the afternoon.

СНАРТER XXXII 

TAIT had spent a sleepless night. The early morning hours were slowly ticking off the seconds until the dawn of another day. Darkness was reluctantly leaving. The quiet solitude of the night had not lessened the pain of his unusual encounter with the girl of his past; nor had it eased the despondent feeling created by his degrading treatment of the previous day. What had he ever done to merit such abasement? The bugle was sounding-men were arising and hustling to make themselves ready for another day's routine. In other quarters Leroy Benton and his wife were engaged in lively conversation. This was the day of their departure and Mrs. Benton felt nervous. She was talking in an excited manner. "I really felt sorry for that young man," she remarked as she pointed toward Corporal Tait, who was sauntering out upon the parade ground. "I suppose you desire a flirtation with him!" Benton's voice was angry. "Not at all, but he seems to be worrying over something, dear." "Undoubtedly, darling. Remember, men don't join this 

Legion unless their conscience hurts them." "I don't believe it is true of him," returned the young wife. "Whatever his trouble, it has aged him greatly." "What of it? They all age here, though. CHAPTER XXXIII 

THE Americans had witnessed their last review. Colonel 

La Tour had taken pride in parading his men before the tourists. The Benton party was just about to depart. 

They had taken their places between two small lines of 

French soldiers. Lieutenant Mercereaux's voice had rung out a command to leave. Mrs. Benton was wrapped in thought. She knew that she must go, yet a feeling, over which she had no control, made her want to stay. She felt as though something had been left unfinished. A man she once knew was here. A decent boy, too! She had destroyed his ideals, she—a woman! Maybe he did like her,—maybe she liked him. Anyway the die was cast and conditions couldn't be remedied. As they awaited the order to leave, she half turned and glanced at Corporal Tait. She turned again and shot one more glance at him-this time she was smiling. Her smile was friendly, and she hadn't meant to do it. Some emotion outside of her control had placed that smile upon her face. A new light of understanding had flashed across her intelligence and Tait seemed a different man now. Had he noticed her friendly smile? At least she hoped he had. At last she knew, that even though he had seen her smile, he was too much of a gentleman to acknowledge it. In this thought, she was comforted. Suddenly, she was aware of her husband. He was nudging her arm and calling in a happy voice! "We're leaving, Tooty, the Lieutenant has just given the command." "Yes, dear. I suppose it must be." "Suppose what must be?" "That-that we must leave." 325

The guests had departed and the Fortress Krim was to experience a new thrill! Corporal Tait had been broken in spirits, but he had seen that last smile, and from it he had gathered strength! Lieutenant Bridwell would not have noticed him ordinarily, he might have thought him a tumble-weed, but now he was alert to every movement or sound. One mistake or oversight could prove fatal to all the men in both companies! 

With crafty eyes, the spy watched the Legion ranks.

Sitting cross-legged on top of the hill he seemed to resemble a small desert pyramid more than anything else. 

In true Arab fashion, his eyes roved over the horizon, as he observed the French approaching. Now they were coming closer. Yes, they would camp on the Plateau Taldemalt, of that he was certain. CHAPTER X X X V 

RANKS had been broken, and a night's encampment on the Taldemalt Oasis was the next thing in order. This fertile place was a delightful spot with a palm tree and a few patches of wiry grass here and there. A fresh water stream found its way down among the rows of tall stately palms. A place in this vast waste of sand which seemed touched by the wand of a fairy. Fresh water in abundance for the men to drink! It had been a long march and the cool drops of mountain water cooled their parched throats. 

This little patch of green seemed to brighten their spirits. 

Maybe it was the freshness of everything which inspired them with a desire to do great things. For ages, Taldemalt had been a half-way stop for caravans. On the north and in the more fertile regions, the Sheik El Kabadahl held sway. His lot was that of prosperity-the tribes under him raised many products. To the south forty or so miles away lay the bad stretches of Sheik Ali Ben Kirzah. 

To this Monarch the poorer parcels of soil belonged. Perhaps it was the geography of the country which had decided the fate, that he should deal in a rich caravan trade instead of becoming a grain raiser. By supplying caravans with their wants, he was enabled to spy upon them and scheme in order to gain possession of the entire desert. 

This then was the position of the two companies camping midway between the camps of two Moroccan chieftains. 

On one side a peaceful camp, on the other a wild tribe, ruled by a devil who was willing and eager to wipe every vestige of civilization from the desert, in order that he might gain a little power. Anything could happen here! 

The légionnaires were busy unpacking their supply train, 336

DEATH SANDS 

337 setting up pup-tents, or leading their horses to water. A voice called softly. "Tait-Tait!" "Yes, who's calling?" "Don't ya recognize me? It's John." "Yes, what's up?" "Come nearer. "I'm here." 99 "I just overheard the Sergeant say the men are goin' to see one sweet time." "When O'Leary speaks-you know well he isn't fooling!

He means that we'll see real fighting-perhaps unexpected fighting, but don't be alarmed, John, you'll come out all right." "I'm not alarmed, chappie, not at all.” "You know a fight might be good for us, we could test our strength." "I'm not itchin' for one, and I know darn well the men aren't. "I-I don't know, John, maybe it's wrong for me to say this; but somehow, you know anything can happen in a fight." "Yer goin' back, see, yer goin' back home. Man, if I ever saw a boob yer it! Ya want to fight-fight. Gosh, I 'ope ya git yer belly full of it." "Maybe you're right.” 99 "'As that girl made ya loco, Tait?” "Perhaps she has, John, and perhaps not." When we git back ta Krim you'll find out what discipline really is. It might be all right to talk in the fortress, but not here! Voices carry in the desert.” "So does the dust from camels." "If—if I didn't need yez, I'd knock yez to the ground so hard ya wouldn't ever git up agin. Go ta yer work." "See the back talk I gave 'im, Tait?" whispered John. "That's me. 'Gentleman John', best all around crook there is. I ain't afraid. Ya see 'ow I 'andled 'im. That's the way ta talk ta them bullies, if ya don't want 'em ta ride ya." Why, if a single idea ever struck that man's brain, the world would die from shock." "Dry up, yez two fools," came the harsh, brutal voice of O'Leary. "Yes, Sergeant. You better keep quiet, John. His nerves are on edge and something might happen." 66 'E wouldn't fight. I know 'im ta well." "The officers seem a little anxious tonight."

339 "Yeah, more than usual. Old Bridwell is pacin' around like a wild 'orse. I bet 'e's never been in many tight places." "Seems like he'd go to pieces quick." "Yeah, and did ya notice 'im today? Sometimes when they're sorry, they do things we don't know about." "Oh, yes, but you don't know her."

Keep quiet, O'Leary's comin' around." "Yer still talkin', yez two? Even the fires are bein' extinguished." "Yeah, the danger must be real." "Maybe it will be yer last meal, boys, so make the best of it- 99 "Ya mean our very last meal, Sergeant?" "That's what I mean. "Not bad-are ya goin' to tell me yer name?" "Don't ask me, it dies with the Legion!"

341 "Let's forgit, then," John's voice rang with disappointment. "Say, do you know the thought just dawned on me that maybe those Berber girls who came to Krim were spies." "They were too dumb; besides, I know women.' "Anyway-John, I feel suspicious of them." "Ah, come on, let's git some rest.” "Yes-looks as though there would be no trouble, so good night." 99 The camp took on an attitude of rest. Every wretch wanted to snatch a few winks of sleep; since they must be up before the break of dawn. Only the lone sentry pacing back and forth broke the stillness of the night. His shadow seemed to keep him company. In another two hours he would be relieved then he could have his rest. The officers had warned him he was in enemy territory, and had equipped him with a whistle to blow in case of trouble.

The sight of this man passing in and out among the palm trees was an unearthly thing to view at such an hour of the night. A breeze was blowing up from the Eastern part of the Taldemalt Plateau-sweeping across the oasis to cool and soothe the aching bodies.

The second sentry had been at his post for just a short time-only a few minutes, in fact, when suddenly the sand hills seemed to be alive with many lights-signaling one to the other. Stopping short in his path, he stood spellbound. Yes, they were Arabs! Hordes of them on every side and as far as his eyes could see! Yet, wait—maybe it was an early morning caravan train, yet why didn't they move over towards the oasis? Perhaps they were resting too. Many thoughts rushed through his brain and tumbled over one another. Again the signal lights! The moonlight disclosed an upraised head-an attack—he was sure of that! 

Quickly blowing his whistle, he awakened the companies. 

Then came the blood-curdling yells of the Berbers!

DEATH SANDS 

"Hey, yez blubbers, wake up, an attack-an you'll 'ave ta defend yerselves." "A fight, Tait!" "Rush for your gun- - !"

The Berbers in their flying robes came rushing down the hills. A myriad of colors-each one yelling his challenge of death-death to the infidel dogs! Tuaregs, the fierce fighting men of the desert led the horde. On-on pushed the blood-thirsty multitude like a pack of hungry wolves! How could a handful of légionnaires cope with these half-crazed Mohammedan Berbers? With super courage, they fought in a frenzy, but the continuous pelting of their guns was as nothing to the Arabs who were drunk with the thought of a sure victory. The French were pushed back and still further back! Kirzah, the fiend of the desert, had struck. He was not satisfied until he had banished all signs of life in the camp. The bloody work was finished! "Come!" yelped Kirzah to his trusty aid, Ben Neubal. "Yes-the work is finished, mighty Sheik, and now the forces at the fort are weakened." "We leave no signs of life here!" "None, Master, the men have searched them all." "You killed all the légionnaires ?" "Yes, every one." "Then it is goodbye, French-for tomorrow I shall seize the fort and be ruler of this desert!" Kirzah's voice seemed weaker and his hand sought his side. "There was one man among them- -a fighting tiger, Master! What's the trouble, are you ill?" "Just a pain in my side. Who was the brave fighter? 

Did you recognize him?" "Corporal Tait-the boy soldier we've heard so much about." "Was he with that body of men ?" "In the very thick of it."

DEATH SANDS 

343 "Too bad to have killed him. I would much rather have taken the lad captive." "For Lola ?" "No, Neubal, as soon as I arrive in camp, the priest will unite Lola and me in marriage." "So you admired Tait?" "From the first I have admired him. He was one of the few brave souls-but I'm tired, Neubal," again he felt his side. "Shall we hurry?" "Yes, we must be on our way-the French are beaten Ha-ha-ha-ha!" 

Kirzah's laugh died almost as soon as it was born. The mighty Chieftain slid to the ground. His garment was soaked in blood from the great wound which Neubal found in his side.

The embers had cooled. Kirzah and his followers were gone, but here in the Oasis of Taldemalt lay the bodies of the brave légionnaires of Krim. However, not all had died in the distance, a half exhausted soldier came crawling over the lifeless bodies-he had broken under this attack and had only escaped death by feigning it. "Tait-Tait! Are yez there? Come on, yez sap!" 

Only a silence greeted him. "I'll find yez, damn itif it takes every ounce of my strength. The boys have all died, hang it, but yez, lad, yer goin' back-goin' back with me!"

In and out among the bodies he crawled until he stumbled over the body of Tait's buddy, John, who had a few weak sparks of life in him even though his arm was severely wounded. "Speak, John, Tait-Tait, where is he?" "Not far from me," came a faint voice. "A little to the right." "Yer all right?” "Yeah, jest the arm-it feels sort of useless. Can't ya bind it up a bit?”

I-I guess he really loved that gal. He died like a mansmiling-smiling and that's not all. He's lookin' at the picture of the girl in his wallet-smilin' at it-smilin', and the girl, she's smilin' too! Stop it-stop it, ya'll drive me mad!" "Yeah, I'll buck up see here! Opposite this girl is an American flag! I guess he loved his country as well as his girl. CHAPTER XXX VI 

"HEY, is there any one alive here?" It was the firm voice of Kabadahl speaking. "Yeah, it's me, Sergeant O'Leary. Who wants ta know ?" "Sheik El Kabadahl, Sergeant, what has happened?" "All the men are killed, Kabadahl, save one other fella." "Ambushed- -?" "Yeah-an the tribesmen left just as quick as they 99 came." "Do you have any idea who made the attack?" "Yes, sir. The Sheik Ali Ben Kirzah and his tribe of cutthroats." “Just what I thought would happen. Come, ride this horse next to me and tell me about it. I'll take you to the fortress. You men assist that injured fellow." "We've already helped him on a horse, Master- "Fine now we hasten to the Fortress Krim. Tell me the story, Sergeant." "It seems as though the Colonel received a message statin' that a fortress was besieged by Arabs." "Some more of Kirzah's dastardly work!" "Yeah-but the message proved true-the trooper that brought it was identified as one of the Legion men- 99 "Only a scheme to draw your men away from Krim." "Exactly, as I see it now. The Colonel, acting in good faith, sent two companies to the relief of their fortress." "How many men- "Eighty in each company, sir." "And you two are the only survivors ?" "Yes, sir." You know, Sheik, I never did think it was one of your men they found outside the fortress walls-I had an idea it was Kirzah's purpose to plant one of his own men there and have him dressed in your colors." "Ah, my man, you don't quite know these deserts. Evil things happen here. I have heard that Colonel La Tour has found his daughter." "She's alive?" "Yes-the old Colonel will be glad to receive her. He has searched for many years, and his heart has been very sad." "Has she reached the fortress ?" "That I don't know. I only heard that she was found."

CHAPTER XXXVII 

On this day, Commandant La Tour was awaiting news of his légionnaires. He had not concerned his mind with the finding of his daughter. Many years ago he had resigned himself to the belief that he would never set eyes on her again. His heart was sad, whenever he spared himself the time to think of her. Little he knew that she was on her way-much nearer indeed than the two survivors of Krim. 

She and her maid could see the huge grim walls of Krim in the distance. This morning they held a new, even a vital interest. On they moved until they came to the entrance gate. The huge door sprung open and Yvette advanced to speak. "Captain Lunier? It is with him I wish to speak.” "Capitaine of the guard?" "Yes, Monsieur." "What do you want? The Colonel has said no more women are to enter the fortress." 

"But Capitaine I am-

DEATH SANDS 

"No, my child, I couldn't have told you. It would probably have meant my life-I intended to lead you back to your father eventually, who is the Colonel here." "But how do you know?" She told me the story of the white Chieftain's daughter who was held captive in the camp." "It may be wrong for me to say, Yvette, but I'm glad that Kirzah is dead. When did you join the secret service ?" "Just before I entered your employ, and when I couldn't bear to be separated from you, I asked permission to accompany you here." 349 took her hand and drew her slowly towards him. Lola was sobbing on his breast. "My baby-my little girl!" Captain Lunier approached the Colonel. "I see Arabs, La Tour!" "Maybe they're coming for me, Yvette?" whispered Lola. "No, my child, they can't take you from your father." "It is Kabadahl,” shouted Captain Lunier.

Kabadahl majestically approached the fortress with the two survivors. "Colonel La Tour, he is here, may I see him?" "Yes, El Kabadahl. I will see you, but what does this mean ?"

"I am the messenger of unfortunate news, Colonel. have brought you two survivors of a tragedy. The two companies were annihilated all save Sergeant O'Leary and Private John. I picked them up in the Oasis. It is a sorry way for me to prove my loyalty to the French. Fate works through mysterious agents." "How right you are, El Kabadahl. As for me, I have always considered you loyal. Are the men wounded?" "O'Leary is all right, but John is wounded."

Lola turned as he spoke John's name. She was in time to see him carried through the gate. With fear in her heart, she rushed to his side and falling on her knees sobbed his name. "John," she cried, "John, my John! It's Lola-your 

Lola !"

"Yeah," whispered John. "Yeah, baby, I get ya. Yer friend Kirzah almost killed me. 'E did get my buddy, Tait, damn 'is 'ide!" "But he isn't my friend. He's dead-your men killed him, John! I'm glad-glad. Do you hear me?" "'E's dead,-we killed 'im,-you're glad? I don't get ya, baby." "I don't belong there any more. I belong here. Colonel 

La Tour is my father, and I love you, John." "Ya love me, baby,-ya mean that? Oh, what a break that is for a guy like me. I love you too, Lola!" 

She bent over him, and their lips met in a lovers' kiss.

Colonel La Tour, the grand old man of the fortress, stood talking to El Kabadahl. Yvette had just told of Kirzah's death. Every now and then his fond eyes would rest on his newly found daughter. "Yes, El Kabadahl," he said, "the desert holds many tragedies. It brought me my wife and daughter. It took them from me,-later it returned my daughter; but my wife is gone-she died in the desert. Kirzah is gone,-two of the French companies are wiped out. The desert indeed claims its victims. Tragic sands-Death Sands!"
